**Islam**[^cite_note-11] is an [Abrahamic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abrahamic) religion based on the [Quran](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Quran) and the [teachings](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sunnah) of [Muhammad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muhammad). The [monotheistic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Monotheistic) religion has an estimated [2 billion worldwide](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islam_by_country) adherents, called [Muslims](19541.md). Islam is the world's [second-largest religious population](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Major_religious_groups) after [Christianity](5211.md).



[al Fatiha (*The Opener*)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Al-Fatiha), a [surah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Surah) from the [Quran](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Quran)

Muslims believe that there is a [primordial faith](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fitra) that was revealed many times through earlier [prophets and messengers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prophets_and_messengers_in_Islam), including [Adam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Adam_in_Islam), [Noah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Noah_in_Islam), [Abraham](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abraham_in_Islam), [Moses](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moses_in_Islam), and [Jesus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jesus_in_Islam), and they believe that Islam is the universal and complete version of this [faith](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Faith). Muslims consider the Quran to be the verbatim word of [God](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/God_in_Islam) and the unaltered, final [revelation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Revelation). Alongside the Quran, Muslims also believe in previous [revelations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamic_holy_books), such as the [*Tawrat*](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Torah_in_Islam) ([Torah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Torah)), the *[Zabur](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zabur)* ([Psalms](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Psalms)), and the [*Injil*](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gospel_in_Islam) ([Gospel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gospel)). They believe that [Muhammad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muhammad_in_Islam) is the main and [final](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Last_prophet) of [God's prophets](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seal_of_the_Prophets), through whom the religion was completed, and after whom no new prophet or divine law will come. The teachings and normative examples of Muhammad, called the [Sunnah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sunnah), documented in accounts called [hadith](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hadith), provide a constitutional model for Muslims. Islam is based on the belief in the oneness and uniqueness of God (*[tawhid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tawhid)*), and belief in an [afterlife](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Afterlife) (*[akhirah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Akhirah)*) with the [Last Judgment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Judgement_Day_in_Islam)—wherein the righteous will be rewarded in paradise (*[jannah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jannah)*) and the unrighteous will be punished in hell (*[jahannam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jahannam)*). The [Five Pillars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Five_Pillars_of_Islam), considered [obligatory](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fard) acts of worship, are the Islamic oath and creed (*[shahada](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shahada)*), daily prayers (*[salah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Salah)*), almsgiving (*[zakat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zakat)*), fasting (*[sawm](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sawm)*) in the month of [Ramadan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ramadan), and a [pilgrimage](23763.md) (*[hajj](23487999.md)*) to [Mecca](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mecca). Islamic law, *[sharia](28840.md)*, touches on virtually every aspect of life, from [banking and finance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamic_banking_and_finance) and [welfare](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zakat) to [men's roles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gender_roles_in_Islam) and [women's roles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Women_in_Islam) and the [environment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamic_ethics#Environmentalism). The two main [religious festivals](2035419.md) are [Eid al-Fitr](154944.md) and [Eid al-Adha](358226.md). The three [holiest sites in Islam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Holiest_sites_in_Islam) are [Masjid al-Haram](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Masjid_al-Haram) in Mecca, [Prophet's Mosque](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prophet's_Mosque) in [Medina](36636.md), and [al-Aqsa Mosque Compound](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Al-Aqsa) in [Jerusalem](16043.md).

The religion of Islam originated in Mecca c. 610 CE. Muslims believe this is when [Muhammad received his first revelation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muhammad's_first_revelation). By the time of his death, most of the [Arabian Peninsula](47858.md) had [converted to Islam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conversion_to_Islam). Muslim rule expanded outside Arabia under the [Rashidun Caliphate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rashidun_Caliphate). The two main [Islamic branches](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamic_schools_and_branches) are [Sunni Islam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sunni_Islam) (87–90%) and [Shia Islam](26961.md) (10–13%).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEPew_Forum_for_Religion_&_Public_Life20091-12] While the [Shia–Sunni divide](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shia%E2%80%93Sunni_divide) initially arose from disagreements over the [succession to Muhammad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Succession_to_Muhammad), they grew to cover a broader dimension, both [theologically](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Schools_of_Islamic_theology) and [juridically](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fiqh). The Sunni canonical hadith collection consists of [the six books](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kutub_al-Sittah), while the Shia canonical hadith collection consists of [the four books](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Four_Books). Muslims make up a majority of the population in 53 countries.[^cite_note-pew2010–2020-9] Approximately 12% of the world's Muslims live [in Indonesia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islam_in_Indonesia), the most populous Muslim-majority country; 31% live [in South Asia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islam_in_South_Asia); 20% live [in the Middle East–North Africa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/MENA#Religion); and 15% live [in sub-Saharan Africa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Religion_in_Sub-Saharan_Africa). Muslim communities are also present [in the Americas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islam_in_the_Americas), [China](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islam_in_China), and [Europe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islam_in_Europe). Muslims are the [world's fastest-growing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muslim_population_growth) major religious group, according to [Pew Research](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pew_Research_Center). This is primarily due to a higher [fertility rate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fertility_rate) and younger age structure compared to other major religions.

## Etymology

See also: [Muslims § Etymology](19541.md#Etymology)

In Arabic, *Islam* ([Arabic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arabic_language): إسلام, [lit.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Literal_translation) 'submission \[to God]')[^cite_note-Schimmel-13][^cite_note-14][^cite_note-15] is the verbal noun of [Form IV](https://en.wiktionary.org/wiki/%D8%A3%D8%B3%D9%84%D9%85#Arabic) originating from the verb سلم (*salama*), from the [triliteral root](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Semitic_root) س-ل-م (*[S-L-M](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/%C5%A0-L-M)*), which forms a large class of words mostly relating to concepts of submission, safeness, and peace.[^cite_note-Lane-Lexicon-4-16] In a religious context, it refers to the total surrender to the will of [God](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/God).[^cite_note-17] A *[Muslim](19541.md)* (مُسْلِم), the word for a follower of Islam,[^cite_note-Lexico-Muslim-18] is the [active participle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Participle) of the same verb form, and means "submitter (to God)" or "one who surrenders (to God)". However, [Quranic studies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Quranic_studies) scholar [Mohsen Goudarzi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mohsen_Goudarzi) has argued that in the Quran the word *[dīn](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/D%C4%ABn)* means "[worship](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Worship)", the *islām* means "[monotheism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Monotheism)" and the *muslim* means "monotheist".[^cite_note-19] In the [Hadith of Gabriel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hadith_of_Gabriel), *Islam* is presented as one part of a triad that also includes *[imān](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iman_\(Islam\))* (faith), and *[ihsān](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ihsan)* (excellence).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEEsposito2000\[httpsarchiveorgdetailsoxfordhistoryofi00johnpage76_76–77\]-20][^cite_note-21]

Islam itself was historically called [*Mohammedanism*](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mohammedan) in the [English-speaking world](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/English-speaking_world). This term has fallen out of use and is sometimes said to be [offensive](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Religious_offence), as it suggests that a human being, rather than God, is central to Muslims' religion.[^cite_note-22]

## Articles of faith

Main articles: [Aqidah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aqidah) and [Iman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iman_\(Islam\))

The Islamic [creed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Creed) (*[aqidah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aqidah)*) requires belief in [six articles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iman_\(Islam\)#The_Six_Articles_of_Faith): God, angels, revelation, prophets, the [Day of Resurrection](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Day_of_Resurrection), and the divine predestination.[^cite_note-23]

### God



Calligraphy showing the word [Allah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Allah) in Arabic in [Hagia Sophia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hagia_Sophia)

Main article: [God in Islam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/God_in_Islam)

The central concept of Islam is *[tawḥīd](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tawhid)* (Arabic: توحيد), the oneness of God. It is usually thought of as a *precise [monotheism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Monotheism)*, but is also [panentheistic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Panentheism) in Islamic mystical teachings.[^cite_note-24][^cite_note-25] God is seen as incomparable and without multiplicity of persons such as in the [Christian Trinity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christian_Trinity), and associating multiplicity to God or attributing God's attributes to others is seen as [idolatry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Idolatry), called [*shirk*](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shirk_\(Islam\)). Thus, Muslims are not [iconodules](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iconodule) and do not attribute forms to God. God is instead described and referred to by several [names or attributes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Names_of_God_in_Islam), the most common being *Ar-Rahmān* (الرحمان) meaning "The Entirely Merciful", and *Ar-Rahīm* (الرحيم) meaning "The Especially Merciful" which are invoked at the beginning of most chapters of the Quran.[^cite_note-26][^cite_note-FOOTNOTECampo200934"Allah"-27] The direct Arabic name for God, *[Allāh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/All%C4%81h)* (الله), a term with no [plural](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Plural) or [gender](38076.md), is also commonly used in other languages by Muslims and Arabic-speaking Christians and Jews to refer to God, whereas *_[ʾilāh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/%CA%BEil%C4%81h)_* (إله) is a term used for a deity or a god in general.[^cite_note-28]

Islam teaches that the creation of everything in the [universe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Universe) was brought into being by God's command as expressed by the wording, "[Be, and it is](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Be,_and_it_is),"[^cite_note-29][^cite_note-Schimmel-13] and that the [purpose of existence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Purpose_of_life) is to worship God.[^cite_note-30] He is viewed as a personal god[^cite_note-Schimmel-13] and no intermediaries, such as [clergy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Clergy), are needed to enable contact with God. Awareness of God is referred to as [taqwa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Taqwa).

### Angels



The Archangel Jibreel giving the Annunciation to Mohammed, from the 1595 illustrated edition of the Siyer-i Nebi. Topkapi Palace Museum, Istanbul, Turkey.

Main article: [Angels in Islam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Angels_in_Islam)

Angels (singular: Arabic: ملك, *_malak_*) are beings described in the Quran[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBurge201523-31] and hadith.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBurge201579-32] They are described as created to worship God and also to serve in other specific duties such as communicating [revelations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Revelation) from God, recording every person's actions, and taking a person's [soul](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soul) at the time of death. They are described as being created variously from 'light' ([*nūr*](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/N%C5%ABr_\(Islam\)))[^cite_note-Encyclopedia-Nur-33][^cite_note-34][^cite_note-35] or 'fire' (*nār*).[^cite_note-36][^cite_note-37][^cite_note-38][^cite_note-39] Islamic angels are often represented in [anthropomorphic forms](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anthropomorphism) combined with [supernatural](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Supernatural) images, such as wings, being of great size or wearing heavenly articles.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBurge201597–99-40][^cite_note-41][^cite_note-42][^cite_note-43] Common characteristics for angels include a lack of bodily needs and desires, such as eating and drinking.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEÇakmak2017140-44] Some of them, such as [Gabriel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gabriel) (*Jibrīl*) and [Michael](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Michael_\(archangel\)) (*Mika'il*), are mentioned by name in the Quran. Angels play a significant role in literature about the [Mi'raj](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Isra_and_Mi'raj), where Muhammad encounters several angels during his journey through the heavens.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBurge201579-32] Further angels have often been featured in [Islamic eschatology](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamic_eschatology), [theology](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kalam) and [philosophy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamic_philosophy).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBurge201522-45]

### Scriptures



A [Quran](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Quran) manuscript resting on a [rehal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rehal_\(book_rest\)), a book rest for the holy text, c. 9th century

Main articles: [Islamic holy books](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamic_holy_books), [Quran](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Quran), and [Wahy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wahy)

See also: [History of the Quran](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_the_Quran)

The pre-eminent holy text of Islam is the [Quran](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Quran). Muslims believe that the verses of the Quran were revealed to Muhammad by God, through the [archangel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Archangel) Gabriel, on multiple occasions between 610 CE[^cite_note-610CE-46][^cite_note-47] and 632, the year Muhammad died.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEEsposito200417–18,_21-48] While Muhammad was alive, these revelations were written down by [his companions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muhammad's_companions), although the primary method of transmission was orally through [memorization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hafiz_\(Quran\)).[^cite_note-49] The Quran is divided into 114 chapters (*[sūrah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/S%C5%ABrah)*) which contain a combined 6,236 verses (*[āyāt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ayah)*). The chronologically earlier chapters, revealed at [Mecca](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mecca), are concerned primarily with spiritual topics, while the later [Medinan](36636.md) chapters discuss more social and legal issues relevant to the Muslim community.[^cite_note-Schimmel-13][^cite_note-Ringgren-50] Muslim jurists consult the *hadith* ('accounts'), or the written record of Muhammad's life, to both supplement the Quran and assist with its interpretation. The science of Quranic commentary and exegesis is known as *[tafsir](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tafsir)*.[^cite_note-51][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEEsposito200479–81-52] In addition to its religious significance, the Quran is widely regarded as the finest work in [Arabic literature](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arabic_literature),[^cite_note-53][^cite_note-54] and has influenced art and the Arabic language.[^cite_note-55]

Islam also holds that God has sent revelations, called *[wahy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wahy)*, to different prophets numerous times throughout history. However, Islam teaches that parts of the previously revealed scriptures, such as the *[Tawrat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tawrat)* ([Torah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Torah)) and the *[Injil](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Injil)* ([Gospel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gospel_in_Islam)), have become [distorted](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tahrif)—either in interpretation, in text, or both,[^cite_note-harvp|Esposito|2002b|pp=4–5-56][^cite_note-harvp|Peters|2003|p=9-57][^cite_note-610CE-46][^cite_note-58] while the Quran (lit. 'Recitation') is viewed as the final, verbatim and unaltered word of God.[^cite_note-Ringgren-50][^cite_note-59][^cite_note-60][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBennett2010101-61]

### Prophets

Main articles: [Prophets and messengers in Islam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prophets_and_messengers_in_Islam), [Sunnah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sunnah), and [Hadith](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hadith)



A 15th-century

[^cite_note-62]

&#x20;[Persian miniature](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Persian_miniature) depicting [Muhammad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muhammad_in_Islam) leading [Abraham](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abraham_in_Islam), [Moses](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moses_in_Islam), [Jesus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jesus_in_Islam) and other prophets in prayer

Prophets (Arabic: أنبياء, *anbiyāʾ*) are believed to have been chosen by God to preach a divine message. Some of these prophets additionally deliver a new book and are called "messengers" (رسول‎, *rasūl*).[^cite_note-63] Muslims believe prophets are human and not divine. All of the prophets are said to have preached the same basic message of Islam – submission to the will of God – to various nations in the past, and this is said to account for many similarities among religions. The Quran recounts the names of numerous figures considered [prophets in Islam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prophets_in_Islam), including [Adam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Adam_in_Islam), [Noah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Noah_in_Islam), [Abraham](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abraham_in_Islam), [Moses](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moses_in_Islam), [David](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/David_in_Islam) and [Jesus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jesus_in_Islam), among others.[^cite_note-Schimmel-13][^cite_note-64] The stories associated with the prophets beyond the Quranic accounts are collected and explored in the *[Qisas al-Anbiya](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Qisas_Al-Anbiya)* (Stories of the Prophets).

Muslims believe that God sent Muhammad as the final prophet ("[Seal of the prophets](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seal_of_the_prophets)") to convey the completed message of Islam.[^cite_note-Esposito-Islam-65][^cite_note-Peters-Allah-66] In Islam, the "normative" example of Muhammad's life is called the *[sunnah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sunnah)* (literally "trodden path"). Muslims are encouraged to emulate Muhammad's moral behaviors in their daily lives, and the sunnah is seen as crucial to guiding interpretation of the Quran.[^cite_note-67][^cite_note-68][^cite_note-69][^cite_note-70] This example is preserved in traditions known as [hadith](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hadith), which are accounts of his words, actions, and personal characteristics. [Hadith Qudsi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hadith_qudsi) is a sub-category of hadith, regarded as God's verbatim words quoted by Muhammad that are not part of the Quran. A hadith involves two elements: a chain of narrators, called [*sanad*](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hadith_studies#Traditional_importance_of_the_sanad), and the actual wording, called *[matn](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hadith_studies)*. There are various methodologies to classify the authenticity of hadiths, with the commonly used grading scale being "authentic" or "correct" (صحيح, *[ṣaḥīḥ](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Authentic_hadith)*); "good" (حسن, *[ḥasan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hasan_\(hadith\))*); or "weak" (ضعيف, *[ḍaʻīf](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Da'if)*), among others. The *[Kutub al-Sittah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kutub_al-Sittah)* are a collection of six books, regarded as the most authentic reports in [Sunni Islam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sunni_Islam). Among them is *[Sahih al-Bukhari](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sahih_al-Bukhari)*, often considered by Sunnis to be one of the most [authentic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hadith_terminology#Terminology_relating_to_the_authenticity_of_a_hadith) sources after the Quran.[^cite_note-71] Another well-known source of hadiths is known as *[The Four Books](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Four_Books)*, which Shias consider as the most authentic hadith reference.[^cite_note-Awliyai-Four-Books-72][^cite_note-Rizvi-Four-Books-73]

### Resurrection and judgment



[Quba Mosque](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Quba_Mosque) is the first mosque to be built in Medina after Muhammed's [emigration (Hijrah)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hijrah) from Mecca

Main article: [Islamic eschatology](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamic_eschatology)

Belief in the "Day of Resurrection" or *[Yawm al-Qiyāmah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Qiyamah)* (Arabic: يوم القيامة) is also crucial for Muslims. It is believed that the time of *Qiyāmah* is preordained by God, but unknown to man. The Quran and the hadith, as well as the commentaries of [scholars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ulama), describe the trials and [tribulations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Tribulation) preceding and during the *Qiyāmah*. The Quran emphasizes [bodily resurrection](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Universal_resurrection), a break from the [pre-Islamic Arabian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pre-Islamic_Arabia) understanding of death.[^cite_note-74][^cite_note-75][^cite_note-76]

On Yawm al-Qiyāmah, Muslims believe all humankind will be judged by their good and bad deeds and consigned to *[Jannah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jannah)* (paradise) or *[Jahannam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jahannam)* (hell).[^cite_note-77] The Quran in [Surat al-Zalzalah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Surat_al-Zalzalah) describes this as: "So whoever does an atom's weight of good will see it. And whoever does an atom's weight of evil will see it." The Quran [lists several sins](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamic_views_of_sin) that can condemn a person to [hell](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hell). However, the Quran makes it clear that God will forgive the sins of those who repent if he wishes. Good deeds, like charity, prayer, and compassion towards animals[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEEsposito2011130-78] will be rewarded with entry to heaven. Muslims view heaven as a place of joy and blessings, with Quranic references describing its features. Mystical traditions in Islam place these heavenly delights in the context of an ecstatic awareness of God.[^cite_note-79][^cite_note-80][^cite_note-81] *Yawm al-Qiyāmah* is also identified in the Quran as *Yawm ad-Dīn* (يوم الدين "Day of Religion");[^cite_note-82] *as-Sāʿah* (الساعة "the Last Hour");[^cite_note-83] and *[al-Qāriʿah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Al-Qaria)* (القارعة "The Clatterer").[^cite_note-84]

### Divine predestination

Main article: [Predestination in Islam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Predestination_in_Islam)

Some Muslims believe in divine predestination, ([Arabic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arabic_language): القضاء والقدر, *_al-qadāʾ wa l-qadar_*) that every matter is decreed by God.[^cite_note-85][^cite_note-86][^cite_note-87][^cite_note-88] The [Muʿtazila](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mu%CA%BFtazila) and certain [Shi'as](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shi'as) do not believe in the concept.[^cite_note-89][^cite_note-90][^cite_note-91][^cite_note-92][^cite_note-93][^cite_note-94] Often, this belief in divine destiny is expressed with the phrase *[Inshallah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Inshallah)*, ([Arabic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arabic_language): إن شاء الله, *if God wills*) when speaking on future events.[^cite_note-95]

## Acts of worship

Main articles: [Five Pillars of Islam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Five_Pillars_of_Islam) and [Ibadah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ibadah)

There are five acts of worship that are considered [duties](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fard)–the [Shahada](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shahada) (declaration of faith), the five daily prayers, [Zakat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zakat) (almsgiving), [fasting during Ramadan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fasting_during_Ramadan), and the [Hajj](23487999.md) pilgrimage–collectively known as "The Pillars of Islam" (*Arkān al-Islām*).\[^cite_note-[www.britannica.com-2023-96](http://www.britannica.com-2023-96/)] In addition, Muslims also perform other optional [supererogatory](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Supererogation) acts that are encouraged but not considered to be duties.[^cite_note-97]

### Declaration of faith



Silver coin of [Akbar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Akbar), the [Mughal Emperor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mughal_Emperor), c. 16th century, inscribed with the *Shahadah*

Main article: [Shahada](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shahada)

The [shahadah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shahada)[^cite_note-FOOTNOTENasr20033,_39,_85,_270–272-98] is an [oath](273418.md) declaring belief in Islam. The expanded statement is "*ʾašhadu ʾal-lā ʾilāha ʾillā-llāhu wa ʾašhadu ʾanna muħammadan rasūlu-llāh*" (أشهد أن لا إله إلا الله وأشهد أن محمداً رسول الله), or, "I testify that there is no [deity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Deity) except [God](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/God_in_Islam) and I testify that Muhammad is the messenger of God."[^cite_note-Mohammad-1985-99] Islam is sometimes argued to have a very simple creed with the shahada being the premise for the rest of the religion. Non-Muslims wishing to [convert to Islam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Convert_to_Islam) are required to recite the shahada in front of witnesses.[^cite_note-100][^cite_note-Galonnier-2021-101]

### Prayer

Main article: [Salah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Salah)

See also: [Mosque](19894.md) and [Jumu'ah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jumu'ah)



Muslim men [prostrating](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prostration) in prayer, at the [Umayyad Mosque](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Umayyad_Mosque)

Prayer in Islam is called [as-salah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Salah) (Arabic: الصلاة), or the more [Persianate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Persianate_society) word *namaaz.* It is seen as a personal communication with God, and consists of repeating units called [rakat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rakat) that include [bowing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ruku) and [prostrating](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sujud) to God. There are five timed prayers each day that are considered duties. The prayers are recited in the Arabic language and performed in [the direction](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Qibla) of the [Kaaba](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kaaba). The act also requires a state of ritual purity achieved by means of either a routine *[wudu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wudu)* ritual wash or, in certain circumstances, a *[ghusl](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ghusl)* full body ritual wash.[^cite_note-102][^cite_note-103][^cite_note-104][^cite_note-105]

A [mosque](19894.md), also called a *masjid* or a *namazgah,* is a [place of worship](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Places_of_worship) for Muslims. Although the primary purpose of the mosque is to serve as a place of prayer, it is also an important social centre for the [Muslim community](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ummah). For example, the [Masjid an-Nabawi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Al-Masjid_an-Nabawi) ("Prophetic Mosque") in Medina, [Saudi Arabia](349303.md), used to also serve as a shelter for the poor.[^cite_note-106] [Minarets](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Minaret) are towers used to call the [adhan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Adhan), a vocal call to signal the prayer time.[^cite_note-Pedersen-Masjid-2010-107][^cite_note-108]

### Almsgiving

Main article: [Zakat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zakat)

See also: [Sadaqah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sadaqah)



A slot for giving zakat at the [Zawiya of Moulay Idris II](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zawiya_of_Moulay_Idris_II) in [Fez, Morocco](39760328.md)

[Zakat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zakat) ([Arabic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arabic_language): زكاة, *zakāh*), also spelled *Zakāt* or *Zakah*, is a type of [almsgiving](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Almsgiving) characterized by the giving of a fixed portion (2.5% annually)[^cite_note-Ahmed-Gianci-2005-109] of [accumulated wealth](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Financial_capital) by those who can afford it to help the poor or needy, such as for freeing captives, those in [debt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bonded_labor), or for (stranded) travellers, and for those employed to collect zakat. It acts as a form of [welfare](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Welfare_spending) in Muslim societies.[^cite_note-110] It is considered a religious obligation that the well-off owe the needy because their wealth is seen as a trust from God's bounty,[^cite_note-111] and is seen as a purification of one's excess wealth.[^cite_note-112] The total annual value contributed due to zakat is 15 times greater than global humanitarian aid donations, using conservative estimates.[^cite_note-113] [Sadaqah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sadaqah), as opposed to Zakat, is a much-encouraged optional charity.[^cite_note-114][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEStefon2010\[httpsarchiveorgdetailsislamicbeliefspr0000stefpage72_72\]-115] A [waqf](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Waqf) is a perpetual [charitable trust](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Charitable_trust), which finances hospitals and schools in Muslim societies.[^cite_note-116]

### Fasting



A fast-breaking feast, known as *[Iftar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iftar)*, is served traditionally with [dates](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Date_\(fruits\)).

Main article: [Fasting in Islam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fasting_in_Islam)

See also: [Fasting during Ramadan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fasting_during_Ramadan)

In Islam, fasting ([Arabic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arabic_language): صوم, *ṣawm*) precludes food and drink, as well as other forms of consumption, such as [smoking](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Smoking_in_Islam), and is performed from dawn to sunset. During the month of [Ramadan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ramadan), it is considered a duty for Muslims to fast.[^cite_note-117] The fast is to encourage a feeling of nearness to God by restraining oneself for God's sake from what is otherwise permissible and to think of the needy. In addition, there are other days, such as the [Day of Arafah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Day_of_Arafah), when fasting is optional.[^cite_note-118]

### Pilgrimage

Main articles: [Hajj](23487999.md) and [Umrah](1591421.md)

See also: [Holiest sites in Islam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Holiest_sites_in_Islam)



Pilgrims at the [Great Mosque of Mecca](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Mosque_of_Mecca) during the [Hajj](23487999.md) season

The Islamic [pilgrimage](23763.md), called the *ḥajj* (Arabic: حج), is to be done at least once a lifetime by every Muslim with the means to do so during the [Islamic month](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamic_calendar) of [Dhu al-Hijjah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dhu_al-Hijjah). Rituals of the Hajj mostly imitate the story of the family of [Abraham](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abraham_in_Islam).[^cite_note-119][^cite_note-120] In [Mecca](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mecca), pilgrims walk seven times around the [Kaaba](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kaaba), which Muslims believe Abraham built as a place of worship, and they walk seven times between Mount [Safa and Marwa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Safa_and_Marwa), recounting the steps of Abraham's wife, [Hagar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hagar), who was looking for water for her baby [Ishmael](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ishmael_in_Islam) in the desert before Mecca developed into a settlement.[^cite_note-121][^cite_note-122][^cite_note-123] The pilgrimage also involves spending a day praying and worshipping in the plain of [Mount Arafat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mount_Arafat) as well as symbolically [stoning the Devil](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stoning_of_the_Devil).[^cite_note-124] All Muslim men wear only two simple white unstitched pieces of cloth called [ihram](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ihram_clothing), intended to bring continuity through generations and uniformity among pilgrims despite class or origin.[^cite_note-125][^cite_note-126] Another form of pilgrimage, [Umrah](1591421.md), is optional and can be undertaken at any time of the year. Other sites of Islamic pilgrimage are [Medina](36636.md), where Muhammad died, as well as [Jerusalem](16043.md), a city of many Islamic prophets and the site of [Al-Aqsa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Al-Aqsa), which was the direction of prayer before Mecca.[^cite_note-127][^cite_note-Trofimov-2008-128]

### Other acts of worship

See also: [Quran § Recitation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Quran#Recitation), [Dua](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dua), and [Dhikr](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dhikr)



Muslim men reading the Quran in the [Umayyad Mosque](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Umayyad_Mosque) of [Damascus](8914.md)

A young man reciting the Qur'an in the [United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islam_in_the_United_States).

Muslims recite and memorize the whole or parts of the Quran as acts of virtue. [Tajwid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tajwid) refers to the set of rules for the proper [elocution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Elocution) of the Quran.[^cite_note-129] Many Muslims recite the whole Quran during the month of Ramadan.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEStefon2010\[httpsarchiveorgdetailsislamicbeliefspr0000stefpage42_42–43\]-130] One who has memorized the whole Quran is called a hafiz ("memorizer"), and hadiths mention that these individuals will be able to intercede for others on Judgment Day.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTENigosian2004\[httpsbooksgooglecombooksidmy7hnALd_NkCpgPA70_70\]-131]

Supplication to God, called in Arabic *duʿāʾ* ([Arabic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arabic_language): دعاء IPA: [\[dʊˈʕæːʔ\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Help\:IPA/Arabic)) has its own etiquette such as [raising hands](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Raising_hands_in_dua) as if begging.[^cite_note-132]

Remembrance of God (ذكر, *Dhikr'*) refers to phrases repeated referencing God. Commonly, this includes Tahmid, declaring [praise be due to God](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alhamdulillah) (الحمد لله, *al-Ḥamdu lillāh*) during prayer or when feeling thankful, [Tasbih](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tasbih), declaring glory to God during prayer or when in awe of something and saying '[in the name of God](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Basmala)' (بسملة, *basmalah*) before starting an act such as eating.[^cite_note-133]

## History

Main article: [History of Islam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_Islam)

For a chronological guide, see [Timeline of the history of Islam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Timeline_of_the_history_of_Islam).

See also: [List of Muslim empires and dynasties](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Muslim_empires_and_dynasties)



A 2004 panoramic view of [Al-Masjid al-Nabawi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Al-Masjid_al-Nabawi) (the Mosque of the Prophet) in [Medina](36636.md), present-day [Saudi Arabia](349303.md). It is considered the second most sacred mosque in Islam

### Muhammad and the beginning of Islam (570–632)

Main articles: [Muhammad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muhammad) and [Muhammad in Islam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muhammad_in_Islam)

For a chronological guide, see [Timeline of early Islamic history](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Timeline_of_early_Islamic_history).

Further information: [Muhammad in Mecca](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muhammad_in_Mecca), [Early social changes under Islam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Early_social_changes_under_Islam), and [First Islamic state](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_Islamic_state)



[Cave of Hira](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jabal_al-Nour)

According to Islamic tradition, Muhammad was born in [Mecca](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mecca) in 570 [CE](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Common_Era) and was orphaned early in life. Growing up as a trader, he became known as the "[trusted one](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Amin_\(name\))" ([Arabic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arabic_language): الامين) and was sought after as an impartial arbitrator. He later married his employer, the businesswoman [Khadija](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Khadija_bint_Khuwaylid).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEEsposito20106-134] In the year 610 CE, troubled by the moral decline and idolatry prevalent in Mecca and seeking seclusion and spiritual contemplation, Muhammad retreated to the [Cave of Hira](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cave_of_Hira) in the mountain [Jabal al-Nour](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jabal_al-Nour), near Mecca. It was during his time in the cave that he is said to have [received the first revelation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muhammad's_first_revelation) of the [Quran](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Quran) from the angel [Gabriel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gabriel).[^cite_note-610CE-46] The event of Muhammad's retreat to the cave and subsequent revelation is known as the "[Night of Power](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Night_of_Power)" (*Laylat al-Qadr*) and is considered a significant event in Islamic history. During the next 22 years of his life, from age 40 onwards, Muhammad continued to receive revelations from God, becoming the last or [seal of the prophets](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seal_of_the_prophets) sent to mankind.[^cite_note-harvp|Esposito|2002b|pp=4–5-56][^cite_note-harvp|Peters|2003|p=9-57][^cite_note-135]



"Muhammad at the Ka'ba" from the *[Siyer-i Nebi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Siyer-i_Nebi)*.

[^cite_note-136]

&#x20;Muhammad is shown with veiled face, c. 1595.

During this time, [while in Mecca, Muhammad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muhammad_in_Mecca) preached first in secret and then in public, imploring his listeners to abandon [polytheism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arab_polytheism) and worship one God. Many [early converts to Islam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Early_Muslims) were women, the poor, foreigners, and slaves like the first [muezzin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muezzin) [Bilal ibn Rabah al-Habashi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bilal_ibn_Rabah_al-Habashi).[^cite_note-137] The Meccan elite felt Muhammad was [destabilizing their social order](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Early_social_changes_under_Islam) by preaching about one God and giving questionable ideas to the poor and slaves because they profited from the pilgrimages to the idols of the Kaaba.[^cite_note-138][^cite_note-139]

After 12 years of the [persecution of Muslims by the Meccans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Persecution_of_Muslims_by_the_Meccans), Muhammad and his [companions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sahaba) performed the *[Hijra](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hegira)* ("emigration") in 622 to the city of Yathrib (current-day Medina). He established the [first Islamic state](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_Islamic_state) there with the Medinan converts (the *[Ansar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ansar_\(Islam\))*) and the Meccan migrants (the *[Muhajirun](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muhajirun)*). The [Constitution of Medina](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constitution_of_Medina) was signed by all the tribes of Medina. This established religious freedoms and freedom to use their own laws among the Muslim and non-Muslim communities as well as an agreement to defend Medina from external threats.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESerjeant19784-140] Meccan forces and their allies lost against the Muslims at the [Battle of Badr](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Badr) in 624 and then fought an inconclusive battle in the [Battle of Uhud](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Uhud)[^cite_note-141] before unsuccessfully besieging Medina in the [Battle of the Trench](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_the_Trench) (March–April 627). In 628, the [Treaty of Hudaybiyyah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Treaty_of_Hudaybiyyah) was signed between Mecca and the Muslims, but it was broken by Mecca two years later. As more tribes converted to Islam, Meccan trade routes were cut off by the Muslims.[^cite_note-142][^cite_note-143] By 629 Muhammad was victorious in the nearly bloodless [conquest of Mecca](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conquest_of_Mecca), and by the time of his death in 632 (at age 62) he had united the [tribes of Arabia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tribes_of_Arabia) into a single religious [polity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Polity).[^cite_note-610CE-46]

### Early Islamic period (632–750)

Further information: [Succession to Muhammad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Succession_to_Muhammad) and [Early Muslim conquests](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Early_Muslim_conquests)

See also: [Ghadir Khumm](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ghadir_Khumm), [Saqifa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saqifa), and [Canonization of Islamic scripture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Canonization_of_Islamic_scripture)



Expansion of Rashidun Caliphate



[Dome of the Rock](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dome_of_the_Rock) in [Jerusalem](16043.md) built by caliph [Abd al-Malik ibn Marwan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abd_al-Malik_ibn_Marwan); completed at the end of the [Second Fitna](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Second_Fitna)

Muhammad died in 632 and the first successors, called [Caliphs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Caliph) – [Abu Bakr](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abu_Bakr), [Umar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Umar), [Uthman ibn al-Affan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uthman_ibn_al-Affan), [Ali ibn Abi Talib](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ali_ibn_Abi_Talib) and sometimes [Hasan ibn Ali](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hasan_ibn_Ali)[^cite_note-144] – are known in Sunni Islam as *al-khulafā' ar-rāshidūn* ("[Rightly Guided Caliphs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rightly_Guided_Caliphs)").[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEEsposito201040-145] Some tribes left Islam and rebelled under leaders who declared themselves new prophets but were crushed by Abu Bakr in the [Ridda wars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ridda_wars).[^cite_note-146][^cite_note-147][^cite_note-148][^cite_note-149][^cite_note-150] Local populations of Jews and indigenous Christians, persecuted as religious minorities and heretics and taxed heavily, often helped Muslims take over their  lands,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEEsposito201038-151] resulting in rapid expansion of the caliphate into the [Persian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sassanid_Empire) and [Byzantine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Byzantine) empires.[^cite_note-152][^cite_note-harvp|Gardet|Jomier|2012-153][^cite_note-154][^cite_note-155] Uthman [was elected in 644](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Election_of_Uthman) and his assassination by rebels led to Ali being elected the next Caliph. In the [First Civil War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_Fitna), Muhammad's widow, [Aisha](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aisha), raised an army against Ali, attempting to avenge the death of Uthman, but was defeated at the [Battle of the Camel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_the_Camel). Ali attempted to remove the governor of Syria, [Mu'awiya](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mu'awiya), who was seen as corrupt. Mu'awiya then declared war on Ali and was defeated in the [Battle of Siffin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Siffin). Ali's decision to arbitrate angered the [Kharijites](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kharijites), an extremist sect, who felt that by not fighting a sinner, Ali became a sinner as well. The Kharijites rebelled and were defeated in the [Battle of Nahrawan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Nahrawan) but a Kharijite assassin later killed Ali. Ali's son, Hasan ibn Ali, was elected Caliph and signed a [peace treaty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hasan%E2%80%93Muawiya_treaty) to avoid further fighting, abdicating to Mu'awiya in return for Mu'awiya not appointing a successor.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHoltLewis197767–72-156] Mu'awiya began the [Umayyad dynasty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Umayyad_dynasty) with the appointment of his son [Yazid I](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yazid_I) as successor, sparking the [Second Civil War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Second_Fitna). During the [Battle of Karbala](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Karbala), [Husayn ibn Ali](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Husayn_ibn_Ali) was killed by Yazid's forces; the event has been [annually commemorated](488563.md) by Shias ever since. Sunnis, led by [Ibn al-Zubayr](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ibn_al-Zubayr) and opposed to a dynastic caliphate, were defeated in the [siege of Mecca](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Siege_of_Mecca_\(692\)). These disputes over leadership would give rise to the [Sunni](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sunni)-[Shia](26961.md) schism,[^cite_note-157] with the Shia believing leadership belongs to Muhammad's family through Ali, called the [ahl al-bayt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ahl_al-bayt).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEWaines200346-158] Abu Bakr's leadership oversaw the beginning of the compilation of the Quran. The Caliph [Umar ibn Abd al-Aziz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Umar_ibn_Abd_al-Aziz) set up the committee, [The Seven Fuqaha of Medina](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Seven_Fuqaha_of_Medina),[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEIsmāʻīl_ibn_ʻUmar_Ibn_Kathīr2012505-159][^cite_note-160] and [Malik ibn Anas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malik_ibn_Anas) wrote one of the earliest books on Islamic jurisprudence, the *[Muwatta](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muwatta_Imam_Malik)*, as a consensus of the opinion of those jurists.[^cite_note-161][^cite_note-162][^cite_note-163] The Kharijites believed there was no compromised middle ground between good and evil, and any Muslim who committed a grave sin would become an unbeliever. The term "kharijites" would also be used to refer to later groups such as [ISIS](9087364.md).[^cite_note-164] The [Murji'ah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Murji'ah) taught that people's righteousness could be judged by God alone. Therefore, wrongdoers might be considered misguided, but not denounced as unbelievers.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBlankinship200843-165] This attitude came to prevail into mainstream Islamic beliefs.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEEsposito201087-166]

The Umayyad dynasty conquered the [Maghreb](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maghreb), the [Iberian Peninsula](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iberian_Peninsula), [Narbonnese Gaul](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gallia_Narbonensis) and [Sindh](28972.md).[^cite_note-167] The [Early Muslim conquests](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Early_Muslim_conquests) have been described as [Islamic imperialism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Imperialism#Islamic).[^cite_note-n050-168] The Umayyads struggled with a lack of legitimacy and relied on a heavily patronized military.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEEsposito201045-169] Since the [jizya](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jizya) tax was a tax paid by non-Muslims which exempted them from military service, the Umayyads denied recognizing the conversion of non-Arabs, as it reduced revenue.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEEsposito201087-166] While the Rashidun Caliphate emphasized austerity, with Umar even requiring an inventory of each official's possessions,[^cite_note-170] Umayyad luxury bred dissatisfaction among the pious.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEEsposito201087-166] The Kharijites led the [Berber Revolt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Berber_Revolt), leading to the first Muslim states independent of the Caliphate. In the [Abbasid Revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abbasid_Revolution), non-Arab converts (*[mawali](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mawali)*), Arab clans pushed aside by the Umayyad clan, and some Shi'a rallied and overthrew the Umayyads, inaugurating the more cosmopolitan Abbasid dynasty in 750.[^cite_note-\:1-171][^cite_note-FOOTNOTELewis199371–83-172]

### Classical era (750–1258)

Further information: [Hadith studies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hadith_studies) and [Islamic philosophy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamic_philosophy)

See also: [Islamic world contributions to Medieval Europe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamic_world_contributions_to_Medieval_Europe) and [Turco-Persian tradition](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Turco-Persian_tradition)

Al-Shafi'i codified a method to determine the reliability of hadith.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTELapidus200286-173] During the early Abbasid era, scholars such as [Muhammad al-Bukhari](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muhammad_al-Bukhari) and [Muslim ibn al-Hajjaj](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muslim_ibn_al-Hajjaj) compiled the major [Sunni hadith collections](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Six_major_Hadith_collections) while scholars like [Al-Kulayni](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muhammad_ibn_Ya'qub_al-Kulayni) and [Ibn Babawayh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ibn_Babawayh) compiled major Shia hadith collections. The four Sunni [Madh'habs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Madh'hab), the Hanafi, Hanbali, Maliki, and Shafi'i, were established around the teachings of [Abū Ḥanīfa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ab%C5%AB_%E1%B8%A4an%C4%ABfa), [Ahmad ibn Hanbal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ahmad_ibn_Hanbal), Malik ibn Anas and [al-Shafi'i](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Al-Shafi'i). In contrast, the teachings of [Ja'far al-Sadiq](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ja'far_al-Sadiq) formed the [Ja'fari jurisprudence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ja'fari_jurisprudence). In the 9th century, [Al-Tabari](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Al-Tabari) completed the first commentary of the Quran, the *[Tafsir al-Tabari](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tafsir_al-Tabari)*, which became one of the most cited commentaries in Sunni Islam. Some Muslims began questioning the piety of indulgence in worldly life and emphasized poverty, humility, and avoidance of sin based on renunciation of bodily desires. Ascetics such as [Hasan al-Basri](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hasan_al-Basri) inspired a movement that would evolve into *tasawwuf* or [Sufism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sufism).[^cite_note-EB-Sufism-174][^cite_note-FOOTNOTELapidus200290,_91-175]

At this time, theological problems, notably on free will, were prominently tackled, with Hasan al Basri holding that although God knows people's actions, good and evil come from abuse of free will and the [devil](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iblis).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBlankinship200838–39-176][^cite_note-178] Greek rationalist philosophy influenced a speculative school of thought known as [Muʿtazila](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mu%CA%BFtazila), who famously advocated the notion of free-will originated by [Wasil ibn Ata](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wasil_ibn_Ata).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBlankinship200850-179] Caliph [Mamun al Rashid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mamun_al_Rashid) made it an official creed and unsuccessfully attempted to force this position on the majority.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEEsposito201088-180] Caliph [Al-Mu'tasim](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Al-Mu'tasim) carried out [inquisitions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mihna), with the traditionalist [Ahmad ibn Hanbal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ahmad_ibn_Hanbal) notably refusing to conform to the Muʿtazila idea that the Quran was [created rather than being eternal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Quranic_createdness), which resulted in him being tortured and kept in an unlit prison cell for nearly thirty months.[^cite_note-181] However, other [schools](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Schools_of_Islamic_theology) of [speculative theology](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kalam) – [Māturīdism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maturidi) founded by [Abu Mansur al-Maturidi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abu_Mansur_al-Maturidi) and [Ash'ari](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ash'ari) founded by [Al-Ash'ari](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Al-Ash'ari) – were more successful in being widely adopted. Philosophers such as [Al-Farabi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Al-Farabi), [Avicenna](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Avicenna) and [Averroes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Averroes) sought to harmonize Aristotle's ideas with the teachings of Islam, similar to later [scholasticism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scholasticism) within [Christianity in Europe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christianity_in_Europe) and [Maimonides](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maimonides)' work within Judaism, while others like [Al-Ghazali](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Al-Ghazali) argued against such [syncretism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Syncretism) and ultimately prevailed.[^cite_note-182][^cite_note-183]



The eye, according to [Hunain ibn Ishaq](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hunain_ibn_Ishaq) from a manuscript dated c. 1200

This era is sometimes called the "[Islamic Golden Age](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamic_Golden_Age)".[^cite_note-184][^cite_note-185][^cite_note-\:1-171][^cite_note-186][^cite_note-harvp|Gardet|Jomier|2012-153] Islamic scientific achievements spanned a wide range of subject areas including [medicine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Medicine_in_the_medieval_Islamic_world), [mathematics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mathematics_in_the_medieval_Islamic_world), [astronomy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Astronomy_in_the_medieval_Islamic_world), and [agriculture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arab_Agricultural_Revolution) as well as [physics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Physics_in_the_medieval_Islamic_world), [economics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_Islamic_economics), [engineering](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_inventions_in_the_medieval_Islamic_world) and [optics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ibn_al-Haytham).[^cite_note-187][^cite_note-Hassan-1996-188][^cite_note-189][^cite_note-190] [Avicenna](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Avicenna) was a pioneer in [experimental medicine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Medical_research),[^cite_note-191][^cite_note-192] and his *[The Canon of Medicine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Canon_of_Medicine)* was used as a standard medicinal text in the Islamic world and [Europe](9239.md) for centuries. [Rhazes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rhazes) was the first to identify the diseases [smallpox](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Smallpox) and [measles](58911.md).[^cite_note-193] [Public hospitals](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Public_hospital) of the time issued the first medical diplomas to license doctors.[^cite_note-194][^cite_note-195] Ibn al-Haytham is regarded as the father of the modern [scientific method](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method) and often referred to as the "world's first true scientist", in particular regarding his work in [optics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Optics).[^cite_note-196][^cite_note-197][^cite_note-198] In engineering, the [Banū Mūsā](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ban%C5%AB_M%C5%ABs%C4%81) brothers' [automatic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Automaton) flute player is considered to have been the first [programmable machine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Program_\(machine\)).[^cite_note-199] In [mathematics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamic_mathematics), the concept of the [algorithm](775.md) is named after [Muhammad ibn Musa al-Khwarizmi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muhammad_ibn_Musa_al-Khwarizmi), who is considered a founder of [algebra](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Algebra), which is named after his book [*al-jabr*](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Compendious_Book_on_Calculation_by_Completion_and_Balancing), while others developed the concept of a [function](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Function_\(mathematics\)).[^cite_note-200] The government paid scientists the equivalent salary of professional athletes today.[^cite_note-201] [Guinness World Records](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Guinness_World_Records) recognizes the [University of Al Karaouine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_Al_Karaouine), founded in 859, as the world's oldest degree-granting university.[^cite_note-202] Many non-Muslims, such as [Christians](18337522.md), [Jews](25955086.md) and [Sabians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sabians),[^cite_note-Brague_2009-203] [contributed to the Islamic civilization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christian_influences_on_the_Islamic_world) in various fields,[^cite_note-Hill-1993-204][^cite_note-Brague-Assyrians-205] and the institution known as the [House of Wisdom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/House_of_Wisdom) employed [Christian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Christian_scientists_and_scholars_of_the_medieval_Islamic_world) and [Persian scholars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_pre-modern_Iranian_scientists_and_scholars) to both translate works into Arabic and to develop new knowledge.[^cite_note-Meri-Bacharach-2006-206][^cite_note-Brague_2009-203][^cite_note-Saliba-1994-207]

Soldiers broke away from the Abbasid empire and established their own dynasties, such as the [Tulunids](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tulunid) in 868 in Egypt[^cite_note-208] and the [Ghaznavid dynasty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ghaznavid) in 977 in Central Asia.[^cite_note-209] In this fragmentation came the [Shi'a Century](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shi'a_Century), roughly between 945 and 1055, which saw the rise of the [millennialist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Millennialist) [Isma'ili](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Isma'ili) Shi'a missionary movement. One Isma'ili group, the [Fatimid dynasty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fatimid_dynasty), took control of North Africa in the 10th century[^cite_note-210] and another Isma'ili group, the [Qarmatians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Qarmatians), sacked Mecca and stole the [Black Stone](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Black_Stone), a rock placed within the Kaaba, in their unsuccessful rebellion.[^cite_note-211] Yet another Isma'ili group, the [Buyid dynasty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Buyid_dynasty), conquered Baghdad and turned the Abbasids into a figurehead monarchy. The Sunni Seljuk dynasty campaigned to [reassert Sunni Islam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sunni_Revival) by promulgating the scholarly opinions of the time, notably with the construction of educational institutions known as [Nizamiyyas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nizamiyya), which are associated with al-Ghazali and [Saadi Shirazi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saadi_Shirazi).[^cite_note-212]

The expansion of the Muslim world continued with religious missions converting [Volga Bulgaria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Volga_Bulgaria) to Islam. The [Delhi Sultanate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Delhi_Sultanate) reached deep into the [Indian subcontinent](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indian_subcontinent) and many converted to Islam,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEArnold1896227–228-213] in particular [low-caste Hindus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dalit) whose descendants make up the vast majority of Indian Muslims.[^cite_note-214] Trade brought many [Muslims to China](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islam_in_China), where they virtually dominated the import and export industry of the [Song dynasty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Song_dynasty).[^cite_note-215] Muslims were recruited as a [governing minority class](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Semu) in the [Yuan dynasty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yuan_dynasty).[^cite_note-216]

### Pre-modern era (1258 – 18th century)

Further information: [Safavid conversion of Iran to Shia Islam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Safavid_conversion_of_Iran_to_Shia_Islam)



[Ghazan Khan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ghazan_Khan), 7

th

&#x20;[Ilkhanate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ilkhanate) ruler of the [Mongol Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mongol_Empire), converts to Islam. 14th-century depiction

Through Muslim trade networks and the activity of Sufi orders,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEArnold1896125–258-217] Islam spread into new areas[^cite_note-218] and Muslims assimilated into new cultures.

Under the [Ottoman Empire](22278.md), Islam spread to [Southeast Europe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Southeast_Europe).[^cite_note-219] Conversion to Islam often involved a degree of [syncretism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Syncretism),[^cite_note-220] as illustrated by Muhammad's appearance in [Hindu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hinduism) folklore.[^cite_note-221] Muslim Turks incorporated elements of [Turkish Shamanism beliefs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tengrism) to Islam.[^cite_note-223][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEÇakmak20171425–1429-224] [Muslims in Ming Dynasty China](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islam_during_the_Ming_dynasty) who were descended from earlier immigrants were assimilated, sometimes through laws mandating assimilation,[^cite_note-225] by adopting Chinese names and [culture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chinese_culture) while [Nanjing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nanjing) became an important centre of Islamic study.[^cite_note-226][^cite_note-227]

Cultural shifts were evident with the decrease in Arab influence after the [Mongol destruction](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mongol_invasions_and_conquests) of the Abbasid Caliphate.[^cite_note-228] The Muslim Mongol Khanates in [Iran](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ilkhanate) and [Central Asia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chagatai_Khanate) benefited from increased cross-cultural access to East Asia under [Mongol rule](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pax_Mongolica) and thus flourished and developed more distinctively from Arab influence, such as the [Timurid Renaissance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Timurid_Renaissance) under the [Timurid dynasty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Timurid_dynasty).[^cite_note-229] [Nasir al-Din al-Tusi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nasir_al-Din_al-Tusi) (1201–1274) proposed the [mathematical model](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tusi_couple) that was later argued to be adopted by [Copernicus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Copernicus) unrevised in his [heliocentric](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Heliocentrism) model,[^cite_note-230] and [Jamshīd al-Kāshī](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jamsh%C4%ABd_al-K%C4%81sh%C4%AB)'s estimate of [pi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pi) would not be surpassed for 180 years.[^cite_note-231]

After the introduction of gunpowder weapons, large and centralized Muslim states consolidated around [gunpowder empires](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gunpowder_empires), these had been previously splintered amongst various territories. The [caliphate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ottoman_Caliphate) was claimed by the [Ottoman dynasty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ottoman_dynasty) of the Ottoman Empire and its claims were strengthened in 1517 as [Selim I](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Selim_I) became the [ruler of Mecca and Medina](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Custodian_of_the_Two_Holy_Mosques).[^cite_note-232] The Shia [Safavid dynasty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Safavid_dynasty) rose to power in 1501 and later conquered all of Iran.[^cite_note-233] In South Asia, [Babur](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Babur) founded the [Mughal Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mughal_Empire).[^cite_note-234]

The religion of the centralized states of the gunpowder empires influenced the religious practice of their constituent populations. A [symbiosis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Symbiosis) between [Ottoman rulers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_sultans_of_the_Ottoman_Empire) and Sufism strongly influenced Islamic reign by the Ottomans from the beginning. The [Mevlevi Order](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mevlevi_Order) and the [Bektashis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bektashis) had a close relation to the sultans,[^cite_note-235] as Sufi-mystical as well as [heterodox](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Heterodox) and [syncretic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Syncretic) approaches to Islam flourished.[^cite_note-236] The often forceful [Safavid conversion of Iran](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Safavid_conversion_of_Iran_to_Shia_Islam) to the Twelver Shia Islam of the Safavid Empire ensured the final dominance of the [Twelver sect](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Twelver) within Shia Islam. Persian migrants to South Asia, as influential bureaucrats and landholders, helped spread Shia Islam, forming some of the largest Shia populations outside Iran.[^cite_note-237] [Nader Shah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nader_Shah), who overthrew the Safavids, attempted to improve relations with Sunnis by propagating the integration of Twelverism into Sunni Islam as a fifth *madhhab*, called Ja'farism,[^cite_note-238] which failed to gain recognition from the Ottomans.[^cite_note-239]

### Modern era (18th–20th centuries)



[Abdülmecid II](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abd%C3%BClmecid_II), the last Caliph from the [Ottoman dynasty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ottoman_dynasty)

Earlier in the 14th century, [Ibn Taymiyya](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ibn_Taymiyya) promoted a [puritanical](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Puritan) form of Islam,[^cite_note-ReferenceA-240] rejecting philosophical approaches in favor of simpler theology,[^cite_note-ReferenceA-240] and called to open the gates of [itjihad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Itjihad) rather than blind [imitation of scholars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Taqlid).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEEsposito2010150-241] He called for a jihad against those he deemed heretics,[^cite_note-242] but his writings only played a marginal role during his lifetime.[^cite_note-243] During the 18th century in Arabia, [Muhammad ibn 'Abd al-Wahhab](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muhammad_ibn_Abd_al-Wahhab), influenced by the works of Ibn Taymiyya and [Ibn al-Qayyim](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ibn_al-Qayyim), founded a movement called [Wahhabi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wahhabi) to return to what he saw as unadultered Islam.[^cite_note-244][^cite_note-ReferenceE-245] He condemned many local Islamic customs, such as visiting the grave of Muhammad or saints, as later [innovations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bidah) and sinful[^cite_note-ReferenceE-245][^cite_note-Musa-2022-246] and destroyed sacred rocks and trees, Sufi shrines, the [tombs of Muhammad and his companions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Destruction_of_early_Islamic_heritage_sites_in_Saudi_Arabia) and the tomb of Husayn at Karbala, a major Shia pilgrimage site.[^cite_note-Musa-2022-246][^cite_note-247][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEEsposito2010146-248] He formed an alliance with the [Saud family](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/House_of_Saud), which, by the 1920s, completed their conquest of the area that would become [Saudi Arabia](349303.md).[^cite_note-Musa-2022-246][^cite_note-249] [Ma Wanfu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ma_Wanfu) and Ma Debao promoted salafist movements in the 19th century such as [Sailaifengye](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sailaifengye) in China after returning from Mecca but were eventually persecuted and forced into hiding by Sufi groups.[^cite_note-250] Other groups sought to reform Sufism rather than reject it, with the [Senusiyya](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Senusiyya) and [Muhammad Ahmad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muhammad_Ahmad) both waging war and establishing states in Libya and Sudan respectively.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEEsposito2010147-251] In India, [Shah Waliullah Dehlawi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shah_Waliullah_Dehlawi) attempted a more conciliatory style against Sufism and influenced the [Deobandi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Deobandi) movement.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEEsposito2010149-252] In response to the Deobandi movement, the [Barelwi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Barelwi) movement was founded as a mass movement, defending popular [Sufism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sufism) and reforming its practices.[^cite_note-253][^cite_note-254]

The [Muslim world](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muslim_world) was generally in political decline starting the 1800s, especially compared to non-Muslim European powers. Earlier, in the 15th century, the [Reconquista](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reconquista) succeeded in ending the [Muslim presence in Iberia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Taifa). By the 19th century, the British [East India Company](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Company_rule_in_India) had formally annexed the [Mughal dynasty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mughal_dynasty) in India.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTELapidus2002358,_378–380,_624-255] As a response to [Western Imperialism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Imperialism), many intellectuals sought to [reform Islam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamic_revival).[^cite_note-256] [Islamic modernism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamic_modernism), initially labelled by Western scholars as [*Salafiyya*](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Salafi_movement), embraced modern values and institutions such as [democracy while being scripture oriented](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islam_and_democracy). Notable forerunners in the movement include [Muhammad 'Abduh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muhammad_Abduh) and [Jamal al-Din al-Afghani](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jamal_al-Din_al-Afghani).[^cite_note-257] [Abul A'la Maududi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abul_A'la_Maududi) helped influence modern [political Islam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Political_Islam).[^cite_note-258][^cite_note-259] Similar to contemporary [codification](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Civil_code), sharia was for the first time partially codified into law in 1869 in the Ottoman Empire's [Mecelle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mecelle) code.[^cite_note-Oxfordref-260]

The [Ottoman Empire dissolved](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fall_of_the_Ottoman_Empire) after [World War I](4764461.md), the [Ottoman Caliphate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ottoman_Caliphate) was [abolished in 1924](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abolition_of_the_Caliphate)[^cite_note-261] and the subsequent [Sharifian Caliphate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sharifian_Caliphate) fell quickly,[^cite_note-262][^cite_note-263][^cite_note-والخلفاء-2023-264] thus leaving Islam without a [Caliph](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Caliphate).[^cite_note-والخلفاء-2023-264] [Pan-Islamists](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pan-Islam) attempted to unify Muslims and competed with growing nationalist forces, such as [pan-Arabism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pan-Arabism).[^cite_note-265][^cite_note-266] The [Organisation of Islamic Cooperation](47725.md) (OIC), consisting of [Muslim-majority countries](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islam_by_country), was established in 1969 after the burning of the [Al-Aqsa Mosque](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Qibli_Mosque) in [Jerusalem](16043.md).[^cite_note-267]

Contact with industrialized nations brought Muslim populations to new areas through economic migration. Many Muslims migrated as indentured servants (mostly from India and Indonesia) to the Caribbean, forming the largest Muslim populations by percentage in the Americas.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHaddadSmith2002271-268] Migration from Syria and Lebanon contributed to the [Muslim population in Latin America](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islam_in_Latin_America).[^cite_note-269] The resulting urbanization and increase in trade in sub-Saharan Africa brought Muslims to settle in new areas and spread their faith,[^cite_note-Pew_Research_Center-2011-270] likely doubling its Muslim population between 1869 and 1914.[^cite_note-271]

### Contemporary era (20th century–present)



Leaders of Muslim countries during session of the [Islamic Summit Conference](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamic_Summit_Conference) in Istanbul, Turkey

Forerunners of Islamic modernism influenced Islamist political movements such as the [Muslim Brotherhood](20742.md) and related parties in the Arab world,[^cite_note-272][^cite_note-273] which performed well in elections following the [Arab Spring](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arab_Spring),[^cite_note-274] [Jamaat-e-Islami](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jamaat-e-Islami) in South Asia and the [AK Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Justice_and_Development_Party_\(Turkey\)), which has democratically been in power in Turkey for decades. In [Iran](14653.md), the [Iranian Revolution](347268.md) replaced a [secular](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Secularism) monarchy with an [Islamic state](2646438.md). Others such as [Sayyid Rashid Rida](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rashid_Rida) broke away from Islamic modernists[^cite_note-275] and pushed against embracing what he saw as Western influence.[^cite_note-276] The group [Islamic State of Iraq and the Levant](9087364.md) would even attempt to recreate the modern gold dinar as their monetary system. While some of those who broke away were [quietist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Political_quietism_in_Islam), others believed in violence against those opposing them, even against other Muslims.[^cite_note-277]

In opposition to Islamic political movements, in 20th-century Turkey, the military carried out [coups](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1997_Turkish_military_memorandum) to oust Islamist governments, and headscarves were legally restricted, as also happened in Tunisia.[^cite_note-278][^cite_note-279] In other places, religious authority was co-opted and is now often seen as puppets of the state. For example, in Saudi Arabia, the state monopolized religious scholarship[^cite_note-280] and, in Egypt, the state nationalized [Al-Azhar University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Al-Azhar_University), previously an independent voice checking state power.[^cite_note-281] Salafism was funded in the Middle East for its quietism.[^cite_note-282] Saudi Arabia campaigned against revolutionary Islamist movements in the Middle East, in opposition to Iran.[^cite_note-283]

Muslim minorities of various ethnicities have been persecuted as a religious group.[^cite_note-284] This has been undertaken by communist forces like the [Khmer Rouge](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Khmer_Rouge), who viewed them as their primary enemy to be exterminated since their religious practice made them stand out from the rest of the population,[^cite_note-285] the [Chinese Communist Party](7175.md) in [Xinjiang](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Xinjiang_internment_camps)[^cite_note-286] and by nationalist forces such as during the [Bosnian genocide](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bosnian_genocide).[^cite_note-287] Myanmar military's [Tatmadaw](20394.md) targeting of [Rohingya Muslims](1918632.md) has been labeled as a crime against humanity by the UN and Amnesty International,[^cite_note-288][^cite_note-289] while the [OHCHR](22463.md) Fact-Finding Mission identified [genocide](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rohingya_genocide), ethnic cleansing, and other crimes against humanity.[^cite_note-290]

The advancement of global communication has facilitated the widespread dissemination of religious knowledge. The adoption of the [hijab](68301.md) has grown more common[^cite_note-291] and some Muslim intellectuals are increasingly striving to separate scriptural Islamic beliefs from cultural traditions.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTENigosian200441-292] Among other groups, this access to information has led to the rise of popular "[televangelist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Televangelist)" preachers, such as [Amr Khaled](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Amr_Khaled), who compete with the traditional [ulema](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ulema) in their reach and have decentralized religious authority.[^cite_note-293][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEEsposito2010263-294] More "individualized" interpretations of Islam[^cite_note-295] notably involve [Liberal Muslims](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liberal_Muslims) who attempt to align religious traditions with contemporary secular governance,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEEsposito2004118–119,_179-296][^cite_note-FOOTNOTERippin2001288-297] an approach that has been criticized by some regarding its compatibility.[^cite_note-298][^cite_note-299] Moreover, secularism is perceived as a foreign ideology imposed by invaders and perpetuated by post-colonial [ruling elites](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ruling_class),[^cite_note-300] and is frequently understood to be equivalent to [anti-religion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Antireligion).[^cite_note-301]

## Demographics

Main articles: [Muslim world](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muslim_world) and [Ummah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ummah)

See also: [Islam by country](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islam_by_country) and [Muslim population growth](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muslim_population_growth)



Global percentage of adherents by religion. Muslims comprise about 25.6% of the world's population, a slightly smaller percentage than Christians.

[^cite_note-Pew_20250609-302]



World percentage of Muslims by country (2010)

[^cite_note-pew2015-303]

: 234–245&#x20;

As of 2020, about 25.6% of the global population, or about 2 billion people, are Muslims.[^cite_note-pew2010–2020-9][^cite_note-pewresearch.orgReligion-304]\[^cite_note-[www.pewresearch.org-2022-305](http://www.pewresearch.org-2022-305/)][^cite_note-306][^cite_note-307][^cite_note-Pew-Growth-2017-308] In 1900, this estimate was 12.3%,[^cite_note-Barrett-2001-309] in 1990 it was 19.9%[^cite_note-Pew_Research_Center-2011-270] and projections suggest the proportion will be 29.7% by 2050.[^cite_note-pew2015-303]<sup>: 8 </sup> A Pew study from 2020 found that the global Muslim population was the fastest-growing religious group over the decade, mainly because Muslims tend to be younger on average and have higher birth rates—two key factors driving natural population growth.[^cite_note-310]<sup>: 9 </sup> According to the 2020 population estimate, 65% of the Muslim population is concentrated in ten countries, with the largest populations residing in [Indonesia](14579.md), [Pakistan](23235.md), [India](14533.md), [Bangladesh](3454.md), [Nigeria](21383.md), [Egypt](8087628.md), [Iran](14653.md), [Turkey](11125639.md), [Sudan](27421.md), and [Algeria](358.md), in descending order. Indonesia is home to over 230,000,000 Muslims, Pakistan over 220,000,000, India over 210,000,000, Bangladesh over 150,000,000, Nigeria over 110,000,000, Egypt over 100,000,000, Iran and Turkey each over 80,000,000, and Sudan and Algeria each over 40,000,000.[^cite_note-311]
