According to a report by [CNN](62028.md), "Islam has drawn converts from all walks of life, most notably African-Americans".[^cite_note-312] In [Britain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islam_in_Britain), around 6,000 people convert to Islam per year and, according to an article in the British Muslims Monthly Survey, the majority of new Muslim converts in Britain were women.[^cite_note-313] According to *The Huffington Post*, "observers estimate that as many as 20,000 Americans convert to Islam annually", most of them being [women](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Women_in_Islam) and [African-Americans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/African-American_Muslims).[^cite_note-314][^cite_note-315]

By both percentage and total numbers, Islam is the world's fastest growing major religious group, and is projected to be the world's largest by the end of the 21st century, surpassing that of [Christianity](5211.md).[^cite_note-316][^cite_note-pew2015-303]<sup>: 14 </sup> It is estimated that, by 2050, the number of Muslims will nearly equal the number of Christians around the world, "due to the young age and high [fertility rate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fertility_rate) of Muslims relative to other religious groups."[^cite_note-pew2015-303]<sup>: 70 </sup>

### Pew Research Center

Pew Research Center estimated in 2009 that 87–90% of Muslims are Sunni and 10–13% are Shia (between 154 and 200 million Shia out of 1.57 billion Muslims as of 2009).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEPew_Forum_for_Religion_&_Public_Life20091-12][^cite_note-Shia2-317][^cite_note-Sunni2-318] Approximately 49 countries are [Muslim-majority](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Muslim_majority_countries),[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEPew_Forum_for_Religion_&_Public_Life200911-319][^cite_note-320][^cite_note-Pew-Landscape-2012-321][^cite_note-322][^cite_note-323] with 62% of the world's Muslims living in Asia, and 683 million adherents in [Indonesia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islam_in_Indonesia),[^cite_note-Pew-Projections-2050-324] [Pakistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islam_in_Pakistan), [India](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islam_in_India), and [Bangladesh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islam_in_Bangladesh) alone.[^cite_note-325][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEPew_Forum_for_Religion_&_Public_Life200915,_17-326][^cite_note-327] [Arab Muslims](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arab_Muslims) form the [largest ethnic group among Muslims](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_contemporary_ethnic_groups) in the world,[^cite_note-Nydell-2005-328] followed by [Bengalis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bengali_Muslims)[^cite_note-329][^cite_note-330] and [Punjabis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Punjabi_Muslims).[^cite_note-331] Most estimates indicate [China](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islam_in_China) has approximately 20 to 30 million Muslims (1.5% to 2% of the population).[^cite_note-332][^cite_note-333] [Islam in Europe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islam_in_Europe) is the second-largest religion after [Christianity](5211.md) in many countries, with growth rates due primarily to immigration and higher birth rates of Muslims in 2005,[^cite_note-334] accounting for 4.9% of all of Europe's population in 2016.[^cite_note-335]

According to the Pew Research Center, [conversion to Islam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conversion_to_Islam) has no major net impact on the Muslim population growth as "the number of people who [become Muslims through conversion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Convert_to_Islam) seems to be roughly equal to the number of Muslims who leave the faith."[^cite_note-336] Although, Islam is expected to experience a modest gain of 3 million through religious conversion between 2010 and 2050, mostly from [Sub Saharan Africa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sub_Saharan_Africa) (2.9 million).[^cite_note-337][^cite_note-338] According to a 2020 Pew study, about 1% of adults raised Muslim leave the faith, while an equal proportion —about 1%— convert to Islam from other religions, resulting in low levels of religious switching both into and out of Islam.[^cite_note-pew2029-339] [Ex-Muslims](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ex-Muslims) are more likely to convert to another religion than to become irreligious.[^cite_note-pew2010–2020-9]

## Main branches or denominations

Main article: [Islamic schools and branches § Main branches or denominations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamic_schools_and_branches#Main_branches_or_denominations)

See also: [Shia–Sunni relations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shia%E2%80%93Sunni_relations)

### Sunni

Main article: [Sunni Islam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sunni_Islam)



The nine volumes of [Sahih Al-Bukhari](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sahih_Al-Bukhari), one of the [six Sunni hadith books](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Al-Kutub_Al-Sittah)

Sunni Islam, or Sunnism, is the name for the largest denomination in Islam.[^cite_note-340][^cite_note-341][^cite_note-Denny-2010-342][^cite_note-343] The term is a contraction of the phrase "ahl as-sunna wa al-jama'at", which means "people of the [sunna](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sunnah) (the traditions of Muhammad) and the community".[^cite_note-344] Sunni Islam is sometimes referred to as "orthodox Islam",[^cite_note-345][^cite_note-346][^cite_note-347] though some scholars view this as inappropriate, and many non-Sunnis may find this offensive.[^cite_note-348] Sunnis, or sometimes Sunnites, believe that the first four caliphs were the rightful successors to Muhammad and primarily reference [six major hadith works](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Al-Kutub_Al-Sittah) for legal matters, while following one of the four traditional schools of jurisprudence: [Hanafi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hanafi), [Hanbali](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hanbali), [Maliki](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maliki) or [Shafi'i](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shafi'i).[^cite_note-Lexico-349][^cite_note-350]

[Traditionalist theology](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Traditionalist_theology_\(Islam\)) is a Sunni school of thought, prominently advocated by [Ahmad ibn Hanbal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ahmad_ibn_Hanbal) (780–855 CE), that is characterized by its adherence to a [textualist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Textualism) understanding of the Quran and the sunnah, the belief that the Quran is uncreated and eternal, and opposition to speculative theology, called [kalam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kalam), in religious and ethical matters.[^cite_note-351] [Maturidism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maturidism), founded by [Abu Mansur al-Maturidi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abu_Mansur_al-Maturidi) (853–944 CE), asserts that scripture is not needed for basic ethics and that *good* and *evil* can be understood by reason alone,[^cite_note-352] but people rely on revelation for matters beyond human comprehension. [Ash'arism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ash'arism), founded by [Al-Ashʿarī](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Al-Ash%CA%BFar%C4%AB) (c. 874–936), holds that ethics can derive just from divine revelation but accepts reason regarding exegetical matters and combines Muʿtazila approaches with traditionalist ideas.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEEsposito1999280-353]

[Salafism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Salafism) is a revival movement advocating the return to the practices of the earliest generations of Muslims. In the 18th century, Muhammad ibn Abd al-Wahhab led a [Salafi movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Salafi_movement), referred by outsiders as [Wahhabism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wahhabism), in modern-day Saudi Arabia.[^cite_note-354] A similar movement called [Ahl al-Hadith](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ahl_al-Hadith) also de-emphasized the centuries' old Sunni legal tradition, preferring to directly follow the Quran and Hadith. The *[Nurcu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nur_movement)* Sunni movement was initiated by [Said Nursi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Said_Nursi) (1877–1960);[^cite_note-Svante_E._Cornell_p._283-355] it incorporates elements of Sufism and science.[^cite_note-Svante_E._Cornell_p._283-355][^cite_note-356]

### Shia

Main article: [Shia Islam](26961.md)



[Nahj al-balagha](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nahj_al-balagha), collection of sermons, letters, and sayings attributed to [Imam Ali](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ali)

Shia Islam is the second-largest Muslim denomination.[^cite_note-357][^cite_note-358][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEPew_Forum_for_Religion_&_Public_Life20091-12] Shias maintain that Muhammad's [successor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Succession_to_Muhammad) as leader, must be from certain descendants of Muhammad's family known as the [Ahl al-Bayt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ahl_al-Bayt) and those leaders, referred to as [Imams](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Imamate_in_Shia_doctrine), have additional spiritual authority.[^cite_note-359][^cite_note-360] Shias are guided by the [Ja'fari school of jurisprudence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ja'fari_school).[^cite_note-361]

According to both Sunni and Shia Muslims, a significant event took place at [Ghadir Khumm](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ghadir_Khumm) during Muhammad's return from his final pilgrimage to Mecca, where he stopped thousands of Muslims in the midday heat.[^cite_note-362] Muhammad appointed his cousin [Ali](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ali) as the executor of his last will and testament, as well as his [Wali](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wali) (authority).[^cite_note-363][^cite_note-FOOTNOTECampo2009257–258-364] Shias recognize that Muhammad designated Ali as his successor (khalīfa) and [Imam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Imamate_in_Shia_doctrine) (spiritual and political leader) after him, but was prevented from succeeding Muhammad as the leader of the [Muslims](19541.md) because of some [other companions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Companions_of_the_Prophet) who selected [Abū Bakr](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abu_Bakr) as [caliph](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Caliphate).[^cite_note-365] Sunnis, instead believe that Muhammad did not appoint a successor before [his death](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Death_of_Muhammad) and consider Abū Bakr to be the first [rightful](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rashidun) caliph after Muhammad.[^cite_note-366] Shias state the community deliberately ignored Ali's nomination,[^cite_note-367] citing [Umar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Umar)'s appointment by Abu Bakr,[^cite_note-368] other historical evidence,[^cite_note-369] and the [Qur'an](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Qur'an)'s stance that majority does not imply legitimacy.[^cite_note-370]

Some of the first Shia Imams are revered by all Shia and Sunnis Muslims, such as Ali and [Husayn](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Husayn_ibn_Ali).[^cite_note-371] [Twelvers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Twelvers), the largest Shia branch and most influential, believe in [Twelve Imams](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Twelve_Imams), the last of whom went into [occultation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Occultation_\(Islam\)) to return one day. They recognize that the prophecy of the Twelve Imams has been foretold in the [Hadith of the Twelve Successors](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hadith_of_the_Twelve_Successors) which is recorded by both Sunni and Shia sources.[^cite_note-372] [Zaydism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zaydism) rejects infallibility of Imams and were sometimes regarded a 'fifth school' of Sunni Islam rather than a Shia denomination.[^cite_note-373][^cite_note-374] They differed with other Shias over the status of the fifth imam and are sometimes known as "Fivers".[^cite_note-375] The [Isma'ilis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ismailism) split with the Twelvers over who was the seventh Imam and have further fragmented into more groups over the status of successive Imams, with the largest group being the [Nizaris](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nizari_Isma'ilism).[^cite_note-376]

For Shias, the [Imam Ali Shrine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Imam_Ali_Shrine) in Najaf, the [Imam Husayn Shrine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Imam_Husayn_Shrine) in Karbala, and the [Fatima Masumeh Shrine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fatima_Masumeh_Shrine) in [Qom](638742.md) are also among the [Islamic Holy sites](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Holiest_sites_in_Islam).[^cite_note-Sardeg2-377]



[Imam Ali Shrine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Imam_Ali_Shrine) in [Najaf](200512.md), a holy site for Shia Muslims



[Imam Hussein Shrine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Imam_Hussein_Shrine) in [Karbala](210957.md), a holy site for Shia Muslims



[Imam Reza shrine](14030191.md), the world's largest mosque, in [Mashhad](406512.md), Iran. 25 million Shias visit the shrine each year.

### Muhakkima

Main articles: [Muhakkima](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muhakkima), [Ibadism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ibadism), and [Kharijites](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kharijites)

[Ibadism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ibadism) is the third-largest branch of Islam whose roots go back to the [Kharijite](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kharijites) secession from the fourth Caliph, Ali.[^cite_note-378] It is practised by 1.45 million Muslims around the world (\~0.08% of all Muslims), most of them in [Oman](22316.md).[^cite_note-379] Ibadism is often associated with and viewed as a moderate variation of the Kharijites, though Ibadis themselves object to this classification. The kharijites were groups that rebelled against Caliph Ali for his acceptance of arbitration with someone they viewed as a sinner. Unlike most kharijite groups, Ibadism does not regard sinful Muslims as unbelievers. Ibadi hadiths, such as the [Jami Sahih](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jami_Sahih) collection, use chains of narrators from early Islamic history they consider trustworthy, but most Ibadi hadiths are also found in standard Sunni collections and contemporary Ibadis often approve of the standard Sunni collections.[^cite_note-380]

An overview of the major sects and madhahib of Islam

An overview of the major sects and *madhahib* of Islam

### Other denominations

- The [Ahmadiyya Movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ahmadiyya_Movement) was founded in British India in 1889 by [Mirza Ghulam Ahmad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mirza_Ghulam_Ahmad) of [Qadian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Qadian), who claimed to be the promised [Messiah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Messiah) ("[Second Coming](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Second_Coming) of [Christ](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jesus_in_Islam)"), the [Mahdi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mahdi) awaited by the Muslims as well as a ["subordinate" prophet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prophethood_\(Ahmadiyya\)) to the Islamic prophet Muhammad.

  [^cite_note-Upal_2021-381]

  [^cite_note-Turner_2003-382]

  &#x20;There are a wide variety of distinct beliefs and teachings of Ahmadis compared to those of *most other* Muslims,

  [^cite_note-Upal_2021-381]

  [^cite_note-Drover_2020-383]

  [^cite_note-384]

  [^cite_note-Turner_2003-382]

  &#x20;which include the interpretation of the Quranic title *[Khatam an-Nabiyyin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Khatam_an-Nabiyyin)*

  [^cite_note-385]

  &#x20;and interpretation of the [Messiah's Second Coming](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jesus_in_Ahmadiyya_Islam).

  [^cite_note-Drover_2020-383]

  [^cite_note-386]

  &#x20;These perceived deviations from normative Islamic thought have resulted in rejection by most Muslims as heretics

  [^cite_note-387]

  &#x20;and [persecution of Ahmadis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Persecution_of_Ahmadis) in various countries,

  [^cite_note-Drover_2020-383]

  &#x20;particularly [Pakistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ahmadiyya_in_Pakistan),

  [^cite_note-Drover_2020-383]

  [^cite_note-388]

  &#x20;where they have been officially declared as non-Muslims by the [Government of Pakistan](1897833.md).

  [^cite_note-389]

  &#x20;The followers of the Ahmadiyya Movement in Islam are divided into two groups: the first being the [Ahmadiyya Muslim Community](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ahmadiyya_Muslim_Community), currently the dominant group, and the [Lahore Ahmadiyya Movement for the Propagation of Islam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lahore_Ahmadiyya_Movement_for_the_Propagation_of_Islam).

  [^cite_note-Drover_2020-383]

- [Alevism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alevism) is a [syncretic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Syncretism) and [heterodox](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Heterodoxy) local Islamic tradition, whose adherents follow the mystical ([bāṭenī](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Batin_\(Islam\))) teachings of Ali and [Haji Bektash Veli](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Haji_Bektash_Veli).

  [^cite_note-390]

  &#x20;Alevism is a blend of traditional 14th-century Turkish beliefs,

  [^cite_note-391]

  &#x20;with possible syncretist origins in [Shamanism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tengrism) and [Animism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Animism) alongside Shia and Sufi beliefs. It has been estimated that there are 10 million to over 20 million (\~0.5–1% of all Muslims) Alevis worldwide.

  [^cite_note-Shindeldecker-Alevi-392]

- [Quranism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Quranism) is a religious movement of Islam based on the belief that Islamic law and guidance should only be based on the [Quran](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Quran) and not the [sunnah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sunnah) or [Hadith](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hadith),

  [^cite_note-393]

  &#x20;with Quranists notably differing in their approach to the [five pillars of Islam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Five_pillars_of_Islam).

  [^cite_note-394]

  &#x20;The movement developed from the 19th century onwards, with thinkers like [Syed Ahmad Khan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Syed_Ahmad_Khan), Abdullah Chakralawi and [Ghulam Ahmed Perwez](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ghulam_Ahmed_Perwez) in India questioning the hadith tradition.

  [^cite_note-395]

  &#x20;In Egypt, [Muhammad Tawfiq Sidqi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muhammad_Tawfiq_Sidqi) penned the article *Islam is the Quran alone* in the magazine *[Al-Manār](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Al-Man%C4%81r_\(magazine\))*, arguing for the sole authority of the Quran.

  [^cite_note-396]

  &#x20;A prominent late 20th century Quranist was [Rashad Khalifa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rashad_Khalifa), an Egyptian-American biochemist who claimed to have discovered a [numerological code in the Quran](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Quran_code), and founded the Quranist organization [United Submitters International](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_Submitters_International).

  [^cite_note-397]

- [Mu'tazilism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mu'tazilism) was an early Islamic theological school known for their use of [rationalism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rationalism), particularly towards the two primary sources of Islam, the [Qur'an](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Qur'an) and the [hadith](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hadith).

  [^cite_note-398]

  [^cite_note-\:0-399]

  &#x20;The school was founded on five main principles: the monotheism ([tawhid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tawhid)), [divine justice](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Divine_justice), the certainty of divine promises and threats, an intermediate position regarding the status of great sinners, and the obligation to enjoin good and forbid evil.

  [^cite_note-\:0-399]

  &#x20;Unlike the Sunnis, Mu'tazilite rejected the traditional view that the Qur'an was the uncreated word of God, and instead held that the [Qur'an was a created revelation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Quranic_createdness), to affirm the absolute oneness of God.

  [^cite_note-400]

  &#x20;Emphasizing the use of reason, they argued that human [free will](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Free_will) allowed individuals to choose between good and evil, making them responsible for their actions.

  [^cite_note-401]

  &#x20;Although the Mu'tazila's influence waned due to social and political pressure from orthodox Sunnis, their rationalist legacy remains important in [Islamic intellectualism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamic_modernity).

  [^cite_note-402]

  [^cite_note-403]

### Non-denominational Muslims

Main article: [Non-denominational Muslim](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Non-denominational_Muslim)

Non-denominational Muslims is an [umbrella term](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Umbrella_term) that has been used for and by Muslims who do not belong to or do not self-identify with a specific [Islamic denomination](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamic_denomination).[^cite_note-404][^cite_note-405] Recent surveys report that large proportions of Muslims in some parts of the world self-identify as solely Muslim, although there is little published analysis available regarding the motivations underlying this response.[^cite_note-406][^cite_note-407] Pew Research reports that respondents self-identifying as solely muslim make up a majority of Muslims in seven countries (and a plurality in three others), with the highest proportion in [Kazakhstan](16642.md) at 74%. At least one in five Muslims in at least 22 countries self-identifies in this way.[^cite_note-Pew-408]

## Mysticism

### Sufism

Main article: [Sufism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sufism)

See also: [Salafi–Sufi relations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Salafi%E2%80%93Sufi_relations)



The Whirling Dervishes, or [Mevlevi Order](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mevlevi_Order) by the tomb of Sufi-mystic [Rumi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rumi)



[Sufism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sufism) in [Konya](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Konya), Turkey

Sufism (Arabic: تصوف, *tasawwuf*), is a mystical [ascetic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ascetic) approach to Islam that seeks to find a direct [personal experience of God](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Divine_presence). Classical Sufi scholars defined *tasawwuf* as "a science whose objective is the reparation of the heart and turning it away from all else but God", through "intuitive and emotional faculties" that one must be trained to use.[^cite_note-409][^cite_note-410][^cite_note-411][^cite_note-412] [Ahmad ibn Ajiba](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ahmad_ibn_Ajiba) defined tasawwuf as "a return to the tradition, and its beginning is knowledge, its middle is action \[upon that knowledge], and its end is a gift \[from Allah]."[^cite_note-413] It is not a sect of Islam, and its adherents belong to the various Muslim denominations. [Isma'ilism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Isma'ilism), whose teachings are rooted in [Gnosticism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gnosticism) and [Neoplatonism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neoplatonism)[^cite_note-Andani-2016-414] as well as by the [Illuminationist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Illuminationism) and [Isfahan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/School_of_Isfahan) schools of Islamic philosophy, has developed mystical interpretations of Islam.[^cite_note-Aminrazavi-2016-415] [Hasan al-Basri](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hasan_al-Basri), the early Sufi ascetic often portrayed as one of the earliest Sufis,[^cite_note-Knysh-2015-416] emphasized fear of failing God's expectations of obedience. In contrast, later prominent Sufis, such as [Mansur Al-Hallaj](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mansur_Al-Hallaj) and [Jalaluddin Rumi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rumi), emphasized religiosity based on love towards God. Such devotion would also have an impact on the arts, with Rumi still one of the bestselling poets in America.[^cite_note-417][^cite_note-418]

Sufis see *tasawwuf* as an inseparable part of Islam.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEChittick20083–4,_11-419] Traditional Sufis, such as [Bayazid Bastami](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bayazid_Bastami), Jalaluddin Rumi, [Haji Bektash Veli](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Haji_Bektash_Veli), [Junaid Baghdadi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Junaid_Baghdadi), and Al-Ghazali, argued for Sufism as being based upon the tenets of Islam and the teachings of the prophet.[^cite_note-420][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEChittick20083–4,_11-419] Historian [Nile Green](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nile_Green) argued that Islam in the medieval period was more or less Sufism.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEPeacock201924,77-421] Followers of the Sunni [revivalist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamic_revival) movement known as [Salafism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Salafism) have viewed popular devotional practices, such as the veneration of Sufi saints, as innovations from the original religion. Salafists have sometimes physically attacked Sufis, leading to a deterioration in [Sufi–Salafi relations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sufi%E2%80%93Salafi_relations).[^cite_note-422]

Sufi congregations form orders (*[tariqa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tariqa)*) centred around a teacher (*[wali](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wali)*) who traces a spiritual chain back to Muhammad.[^cite_note-423] Sufis played an important role in the formation of Muslim societies through their missionary and educational activities.[^cite_note-EB-Sufism-174] The Sufism-influenced Ahle Sunnat movement or [Barelvi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Barelvi) movement claims over 200 million followers in South Asia.[^cite_note-424][^cite_note-425][^cite_note-426] Sufism is prominent in Central Asia,[^cite_note-427][^cite_note-428] as well as in African countries like [Tunisia](30188.md), [Algeria](358.md), [Morocco](19291.md), [Senegal](27278.md), [Chad](5488.md) and [Niger](21373.md).[^cite_note-Pew-408][^cite_note-429]

## Law and jurisprudence

### Sharia

Main articles: [Sharia](28840.md) and [Fiqh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fiqh)

See also: [Logic in Islamic philosophy § Islamic law and theology](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Logic_in_Islamic_philosophy#Islamic_law_and_theology)



Islamic [schools of law](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Madh'hab) in the [Muslim world](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muslim_world)

[Sharia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shariah) is the body of Islamic [religious law](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Religious_law).[^cite_note-Lexico-349][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEEsposito2002b17,_111–112,_118-430] The desire to delineate and discover laws in a comprehensive and consistent method led to the development of the [theory of law](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jurisprudence), called [fiqh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fiqh).[^cite_note-431][^cite_note-vikor-432] Conversely, [bid'ah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bid'ah) is used to refer to unlawful innovations in matters of religion.[^cite_note-433] Differing methodologies, called principles of fiqh or *[Usul al-fiqh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Usul_al-fiqh)*, have developed and a school of jurisprudence arising around a methodology is known as a *madhhab* ([Arabic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arabic_language): مذهب). The conformity in following of decisions by a religious expert or school is called *[taqlid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Taqlid)*. The term *[ghayr muqallid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Salafi_movement#Views_on_Taqlid_\(adherence_to_legal_precedent\))* refers to those who do not use taqlid and, by extension, do not have a madhab.[^cite_note-434] The practice of an individual interpreting law with independent reasoning is called *[ijtihad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ijtihad)*.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEWeiss20023,_161-435][^cite_note-ODI-436] Those who interpret shariah are known as [muftis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mufti) and their legal opinions are called [fatwas](11259.md).[^cite_note-ODI-436][^cite_note-vikor-432]

The primary sources of Shariah are the Quran and Sunnah.[^cite_note-437] A common third source is [qiyas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Qiyas) (analogical reasoning) which is used for legal questions not dealt with literally in the Qur’ān or Sunnah. Parallels would be searched for to find the *illah*, or effective cause, which is the reason behind the existing ruling.[^cite_note-438] For example, from the specific prohibition of wine is deduced a broad prohibition on alcohol as they share the operative cause identified as the mind-altering nature of all alcoholic drinks.[^cite_note-439][^cite_note-440] The [Zahiri school](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zahiri_school) adheres to strict literalism and thus rejects qiyas. Consensus of opinion is [ijma](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ijma), while [ikhtilaf](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ikhtilaf) refers to scholarly disagreement. Rulings assign actions to one of five categories called [ahkam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ahkam): mandatory (*[fard](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fard)*), recommended (*[mustahabb](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mustahabb)*), permitted (*[mubah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mubah)*), abhorred (*[makruh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Makruh)*), and prohibited (*[haram](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Haram)*).[^cite_note-ODI-436][^cite_note-vikor-432]

In the modern era, sharia-based criminal laws were widely replaced by statutes inspired by European models.[^cite_note-vikor-432] The [Ottoman Empire](22278.md)'s 19th-century [Tanzimat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tanzimat) reforms led to the [Mecelle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mecelle) civil code and represented the first attempt to [codify](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Codification_\(law\)) sharia.[^cite_note-Oxfordref-260] While the constitutions of most Muslim-majority states contain references to sharia, its classical rules were largely retained only in [personal status](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Status_\(law\)) (family) laws.[^cite_note-vikor-432] Legislative bodies which codified these laws sought to modernize them without abandoning their foundations in traditional jurisprudence.[^cite_note-vikor-432][^cite_note-mayer-441] The [Islamic revival](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamic_revival) of the late 20th century brought along calls by [Islamist](15012.md) movements for complete implementation of sharia.[^cite_note-vikor-432][^cite_note-mayer-441] The role of sharia has become a contested topic around the world. There are ongoing debates as to whether sharia is compatible with secular forms of government, human rights, [freedom of thought](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Freedom_of_thought), and [women's rights](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Women's_rights) due to concerns including on [censorship](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Religious_censorship#In_Islam) and [violence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islam_and_violence).[^cite_note-442][^cite_note-443]

## Society

### Religious personages

Main article: [Ulama](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ulama)



[Crimean Tatar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crimean_Tatars) Muslim students (1856)

Islam has no clergy in the [sacerdotal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sacerdotalism) sense, such as priests who mediate between God and people. *[Imam](15240.md)* (إمام) is the religious title used to refer to an Islamic leadership position, often in the context of conducting an Islamic worship service.[^cite_note-444] Religious interpretation is presided over by the '*ulama* (Arabic: علماء), a term used describe the body of Muslim scholars who have received training in [Islamic studies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamic_studies). A scholar of the hadith is called a *[muhaddith](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muhaddith)*, a scholar of jurisprudence is called a *[faqih](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Faqih)* (فقيه), a jurist who is qualified to issue legal opinions or *[fatwas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fatwas)* is called a [mufti](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mufti), and a *[qadi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Qadi)* is an Islamic judge. [Honorific](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Honorific) titles given to scholars include [sheikh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sheikh), [mullah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mullah) and *[mawlawi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mawlawi_\(Islamic_title\))*. Some Muslims also venerate [saints](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saints_in_Islam) associated with [miracles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamic_view_of_miracles) (كرامات, *karāmāt*).[^cite_note-445]

### Governance

See also: [Politics and Islam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Politics_and_Islam), [Islamic economics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamic_economics), [Islamic military jurisprudence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamic_military_jurisprudence), [tasamuh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tasamuh), and [Jihad](16203.md)

In [Islamic economic jurisprudence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamic_economic_jurisprudence), hoarding of wealth is reviled and thus [monopolistic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Monopoly) behavior is frowned upon.[^cite_note-Iqbal2010-446] Attempts to comply with sharia has led to the development of [Islamic banking](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamic_banking). Islam prohibits *[riba](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Riba)*, usually translated as [usury](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Usury), which refers to any unfair gain in trade and is most commonly used to mean [interest](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Interest).[^cite_note-447] Instead, Islamic banks go into partnership with the borrower, and both share from the profits and any losses from the venture. Another feature is the avoidance of uncertainty, which is seen as gambling[^cite_note-448] and Islamic banks traditionally avoid derivative instruments such as futures or options which has historically protected them from market downturns.[^cite_note-449] The Rashidun and Umayyad Caliphate used to be involved in distribution of charity from the treasury, known as [Bayt al-mal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bayt_al-mal), before it became a largely individual pursuit around the year 720. The first [Caliph](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Caliph), [Abu Bakr](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abu_Bakr), distributed zakat as one of the first examples of a [guaranteed minimum income](457460.md), with each citizen getting 10 to 20 [dirhams](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dirhams) annually.[^cite_note-450] During the reign of the second Caliph Umar, [child support](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Child_support) was introduced and the old and disabled were entitled to stipends,[^cite_note-451][^cite_note-452] while the Umayyad Caliph [Umar II](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Umar_II) assigned a servant for each blind person and for every two chronically ill persons.[^cite_note-453]

[Jihad](16203.md) means "to strive or struggle \[in the way of God]" and, in its broadest sense, is "exerting one's utmost power, efforts, endeavors, or ability in contending with an object of [disapprobation](https://en.wiktionary.org/wiki/disapprobation)".[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEFirestone199917–18-454] Shias in particular emphasize the "greater jihad" of striving to attain spiritual [self-perfection](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Self-improvement)[^cite_note-Afsaruddin-455][^cite_note-456][^cite_note-457] while the "lesser jihad" is defined as warfare.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEFirestone199917-458][^cite_note-EI2-459] When used without a qualifier, jihad is often understood in its military form.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEFirestone199917–18-454][^cite_note-Afsaruddin-455] Jihad is the only form of warfare permissible in Islamic law and may be declared against illegal works, terrorists, criminal groups, rebels, [apostates](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Apostasy_in_Islam), and leaders or states who oppress Muslims.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEFirestone199917-458][^cite_note-EI2-459] Most Muslims today interpret Jihad as only a defensive form of warfare.[^cite_note-Habeck2006-460] Jihad only becomes an individual duty for those vested with authority. For the rest of the populace, this happens only in the case of a [general mobilization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/General_mobilization).[^cite_note-EI2-459] For most [Twelver Shias](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Twelver), [offensive jihad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Offensive_jihad) can only be declared by a [divinely appointed leader](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Imamate_in_Twelver_doctrine) of the Muslim community, and as such, is suspended since [Muhammad al-Mahdi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muhammad_al-Mahdi)'s [occultation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Occultation_\(Islam\)) in 868 CE.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESachedina1998105–106-461][^cite_note-FOOTNOTENasr200372-462]

### Daily and family life

See also: [Adab (Islam)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Adab_\(Islam\)), [Islamic dietary laws](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamic_dietary_laws), [Islam and children](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islam_and_children), [Marriage in Islam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marriage_in_Islam), [Women in Islam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Women_in_Islam), and [Polygyny in Islam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Polygyny_in_Islam)



A woman wearing an [Islamic veil](68301.md)

Many daily practices fall in the category of *adab*, or etiquette. Specific prohibited foods include pork products, blood and [carrion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carrion). Health is viewed as a trust from God and [intoxicants](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Khamr), such as [alcoholic drinks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alcoholic_drink), are prohibited.[^cite_note-463] All meat must come from a [herbivorous](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Herbivorous) animal slaughtered in the name of God by a Muslim, Jew, or Christian, except for game that one has hunted or fished for oneself.[^cite_note-464][^cite_note-465][^cite_note-466] Beards are often encouraged among men as something natural[^cite_note-467] and body modifications, such as [permanent tattoos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Religious_perspectives_on_tattooing#Islam), are usually forbidden as violating the creation.[^cite_note-469][^cite_note-470] Silk and gold are prohibited for men in Islam to maintain a state of sobriety.[^cite_note-471] *[Haya](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Haya_\(Islam\))*, often translated as "shame" or "modesty", is sometimes described as the innate character of Islam[^cite_note-472] and informs much of Muslim daily life. For example, [clothing in Islam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamic_clothing) emphasizes a standard of modesty, which has included the *[hijab](68301.md)* for women. Similarly, [personal hygiene](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamic_hygienical_jurisprudence) is encouraged with certain requirements.[^cite_note-473]



A Muslim couple

In [Islamic marriage](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marriage_in_Islam), the groom is required to pay a bridal gift (*[mahr](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mahr)*).[^cite_note-474][^cite_note-475][^cite_note-476] Most families in the Islamic world are monogamous.[^cite_note-477][^cite_note-478] Muslim men are allowed to practice [polygyny](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Polygyny) and can have up to four wives simultaneously. Islamic teachings strongly advise that if a man cannot ensure equal financial and emotional support for each of his wives, it is recommended that he marry just one woman. One reason cited for polygyny is that it allows a man to give financial protection to multiple women, who might otherwise not have any support (e.g. widows). However, the first wife can set a condition in the marriage contract that the husband cannot marry another woman during their marriage.[^cite_note-479][^cite_note-480] There are also cultural variations in weddings.[^cite_note-481] [Polyandry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Polyandry), a practice wherein a woman takes on two or more husbands, is prohibited in Islam.[^cite_note-482]



[Muslim](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muslim) girls studying the [Quran](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Quran) placed atop folding [lecterns](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lectern) ([*rehal*](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rehal_\(book_rest\))) during [Ramadan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ramadan) in [Qom](638742.md), Iran

After the birth of a child, the *[adhan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Adhan)* is pronounced in the right ear.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECampo2009106-483] On the seventh day, the *[aqiqah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aqiqah)* ceremony is performed, in which an animal is sacrificed and its meat is distributed among the poor.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTENigosian2004\[httpsbooksgooglecombooksidmy7hnALd_NkCpgPA120_120\]-484] The child's head is shaved, and an amount of money equaling the weight of its hair is donated to the poor.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTENigosian2004\[httpsbooksgooglecombooksidmy7hnALd_NkCpgPA120_120\]-484] Male [circumcision](8718425.md), called *[khitan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Khitan_\(circumcision\))*,[^cite_note-485] is often practised in the Muslim world.[^cite_note-486][^cite_note-487] Respecting and obeying one's parents, and taking care of them especially in their old age is a religious obligation.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECampo2009136-488]

A [dying Muslim](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamic_view_of_death) is encouraged to pronounce the *Shahada* as their last words.[^cite_note-489] Paying respects to the dead and attending funerals in the community are considered among the virtuous acts. In [Islamic burial rituals](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamic_funeral), burial is encouraged as soon as possible, usually within 24 hours. The body is washed, except for martyrs, by members of the same gender and enshrouded in a garment that must not be elaborate called *kafan*.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEStefon2010\[httpsarchiveorgdetailsislamicbeliefspr0000stefpage83_83\]-490] A "funeral prayer" called *[Salat al-Janazah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Salat_al-Janazah)* is performed. Wailing or loud, mournful outcrying, is discouraged. Coffins are often not preferred and graves are often unmarked, even for kings.[^cite_note-491]

### Arts and culture

Main article: [Islamic culture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamic_culture)

See also: [Islamic art](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamic_art), [Islamic architecture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamic_architecture), [Islamic literature](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamic_literature), [Islam and association football](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islam_and_association_football), and [Cultural Muslims](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cultural_Muslims)

The term "[Islamic culture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamic_culture)" can be used to mean aspects of culture that pertain to the religion, such as [festivals](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Festivals) and [dress code](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamic_clothing). It is also controversially used to denote the cultural aspects of traditionally Muslim people.[^cite_note-492] Finally, "Islamic civilization" may also refer to the aspects of the synthesized culture of the early Caliphates, including that of non-Muslims,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEEsposito201056-493] sometimes referred to as "[Islamicate](https://en.wiktionary.org/wiki/Islamicate)".[^cite_note-494]

[Islamic art](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamic_art) encompasses the [visual arts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Visual_arts) including fields as varied as [architecture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamic_architecture), [calligraphy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamic_calligraphy), painting, and [ceramics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamic_ceramics), among others.[^cite_note-495][^cite_note-496] While the making of images of animate beings has often been frowned upon in connection with [laws against idolatry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aniconism_in_Islam), this rule has been interpreted in different ways by different scholars and in different historical periods. This stricture has been used to explain the prevalence of calligraphy, [tessellation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tessellation), and pattern as key aspects of Islamic artistic culture.[^cite_note-497] Additionally, the [depiction of Muhammad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Depictions_of_Muhammad) is a contentious issue among Muslims.[^cite_note-498] In Islamic architecture, varying cultures show influence such as North African and Spanish Islamic architecture such as the [Great Mosque of Kairouan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Mosque_of_Kairouan) containing [marble](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marble) and [porphyry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Porphyry_\(geology\)) columns from Roman and Byzantine buildings,[^cite_note-499] while [mosques in Indonesia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mosques_in_Indonesia) often have multi-tiered roofs from local [Javanese](69336.md) styles.[^cite_note-500]

The [Islamic calendar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamic_calendar) is a [lunar calendar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lunar_calendar) that begins with the [Hijra](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hegira) of 622 CE, a date that was reportedly chosen by Caliph Umar as it was an important turning point in Muhammad's fortunes.[^cite_note-501] Islamic [holy days](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muslim_holidays) fall on fixed dates of the lunar calendar, meaning they occur in [different seasons](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seasons) in different years in the [Gregorian calendar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gregorian_calendar). The most important Islamic festivals are *[Eid al-Fitr](154944.md)* ([Arabic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arabic_language): عيد الفطر) on the 1st of *[Shawwal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shawwal)*, marking the end of the fasting month *Ramadan*, and *[Eid al-Adha](358226.md)* (عيد الأضحى) on the 10th of *Dhu al-Hijjah*, coinciding with the end of the [Hajj](23487999.md) (pilgrimage).[^cite_note-502]\[^cite_note-[www.britannica.com-2023-96](http://www.britannica.com-2023-96/)]

[Cultural Muslims](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cultural_Muslims) are religiously non-practicing individuals who still identify with Islam due to family backgrounds, personal experiences, or the social and cultural environment in which they grew up.[^cite_note-503][^cite_note-504]

## Islamist movements

Main article: [Islamism](15012.md)

See also: [Islamism by country](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamism_by_country)

[Islamism](15012.md) is a range of [religious](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Religion) and [political](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Politics) ideological movements that believe that Islam should influence political systems; among the most prominent are the [Khilafat Movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Khilafat_Movement), [Islamic revival](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamic_revival), [Islamic democracy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamic_democracy), the [Deobandi movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Deobandi_movement), the [Salafi movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Salafi_movement), among others.[^cite_note-505] Its proponents believe Islam is innately political, and that Islam as a political system is superior to [communism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Communism), [liberal democracy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liberal_democracy), [capitalism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Capitalism), and other alternatives in achieving a just, successful society.[^cite_note-506] The advocates of Islamism, also known as "al-Islamiyyun", are usually affiliated with Islamic institutions or social mobilization movements,[^cite_note-507] emphasizing the implementation of *[sharia](28840.md)*,[^cite_note-eikmeier-2007-508] [pan-Islamic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pan-Islamic) political unity,[^cite_note-eikmeier-2007-508] and the creation of [Islamic states](2646438.md).[^cite_note-509]

## Influences on other religions

Whether movements such as the [Druze](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Druze),[^cite_note-510][^cite_note-511][^cite_note-512] [Berghouata](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Berghouata) and [Ha-Mim](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ha-Mim), either emerged from Islam or came to share certain beliefs with Islam, and whether each is a separate religion or a sect of Islam are controversial viewpoints.[^cite_note-513] The Druze faith further split from [Isma'ilism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Isma'ilism) as it developed its own unique doctrines, and finally separated from both Ismāʿīlīsm and Islam altogether; these include the belief that the Imam [Al-Ḥākim bi-Amr Allāh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Al-Hakim_bi-Amr_Allah) was [God incarnate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Incarnation).[^cite_note-514][^cite_note-515] [Yazdânism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yazd%C3%A2nism) is seen as a blend of local Kurdish beliefs and Islamic Sufi doctrine introduced to [Kurdistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kurdistan) by [Sheikh Adi ibn Musafir](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sheikh_Adi_ibn_Musafir) in the 12th century.[^cite_note-516] [Bábism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/B%C3%A1bism) stems from Twelver Shia passed through [Siyyid 'Ali Muhammad i-Shirazi al-Bab](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Siyyid_'Ali_Muhammad_i-Shirazi_al-Bab) while one of his followers Mirza Husayn 'Ali Nuri [Baha'u'llah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Baha'u'llah) founded the [Baháʼí Faith](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bah%C3%A1%CA%BC%C3%AD_Faith).[^cite_note-517] [Yarsanism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yarsanism),[^cite_note-Z._Mir-Hosseini_1994,_p.267-518] [Din-i Ilahi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Din-i_Ilahi),[^cite_note-FOOTNOTERoychoudhury1941306-519] and [Ali-Illahism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ali-Illahism)[^cite_note-520] are considered separations from Islam. [Sikhism](27964.md), founded by [Guru Nanak](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Guru_Nanak) in [Punjab](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Punjab) in the late 15th century, was influenced by Islam.[^cite_note-521]

## Criticism

Main article: [Criticism of Islam](2394308.md)

See also: [Criticism of Muhammad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Criticism_of_Muhammad) and [Criticism of the Quran](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Criticism_of_the_Quran)



[John of Damascus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_of_Damascus), under the [Umayyad Caliphate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Umayyad_Caliphate), viewed Islamic doctrines as a hodgepodge from the [Bible](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bible).

[^cite_note-522]

Criticism of Islam has existed since its formative stages. Early criticism came from Jewish authors, such as [Ibn Kammuna](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ibn_Kammuna), and Christian authors, many of whom viewed Islam as a [Christian heresy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Medieval_Christian_views_on_Muhammad) or a form of [idolatry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Idolatry), often explaining it in [apocalyptic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Apocalypticism) terms.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEFahlbusch_et_al2001\[httpsbooksgooglecombooksidyaecVMhMWaECpgPA759_759\]-523] Christian writers criticized Islam's sensual descriptions of paradise. Ninth-century Muslim scholar [Ali ibn Sahl Rabban al-Tabari](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ali_ibn_Sahl_Rabban_al-Tabari) defended the Quranic description of paradise by asserting that the Bible also implies such ideas, such as drinking wine in heaven in the [Gospel of Matthew](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gospel_of_Matthew). Christian 4th-century theologian [Augustine of Hippo](2030.md)'s doctrines led to the broad repudiation of bodily pleasure in both life and the afterlife.[^cite_note-524]

Defamatory images of [Muhammad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Medieval_Christian_views_on_Muhammad), derived from early 7th-century depictions of the [Byzantine Church](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eastern_Orthodox_Church_in_the_Byzantine_Empire),[^cite_note-525] appear in the 14th-century epic poem *[Divine Comedy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Divine_Comedy)* by [Dante Alighieri](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dante_Alighieri).[^cite_note-Stone-526] Here, Muhammad is depicted in the eighth circle of hell, along with Ali. Dante does not blame Islam as a whole but accuses Muhammad of [schism](6288753.md), by establishing another religion after Christianity.[^cite_note-Stone-526]

Other criticisms centre on the [treatment of individuals within modern Muslim-majority countries](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Human_rights_in_Muslim-majority_countries), including issues related to human rights, particularly in relation to the application of Islamic law.[^cite_note-527] Furthermore, in the wake of recent [multiculturalism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Multiculturalism) trends, the supposed ability of [Muslim immigrants](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muslim_diaspora) in the West to assimilate has been [criticized](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Criticism_of_multiculturalism) and blamed upon Islamic backgrounds by some.[^cite_note-528] A study found higher Islamic religiosity correlated with higher [Islamic extremism](8734632.md).[^cite_note-529]

'submission \[to the [will of God](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Will_of_God)]'

[^cite_note-178]: &#x20;"Hasan al Basri is often considered one of the first who rejected an angelic origin for the devil, arguing that his fall was the result of his own free-will, not God's determination. Hasan al Basri also argued that angels are incapable of sin or errors and nobler than humans and even prophets. Both early Shias and Sunnis opposed his view.<sup>[\[172\]](#cite_note-177)</sup>

[^cite_note-257]: &#x20;Lauziere, Henri (2016). *The Making of Salafism: Islamic Reform in the Twentieth Century*. New York, Chichester, West Sussex: Columbia University Press. pp. 231–232. [ISBN](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ISBN_\(identifier\)) [978-0-231-17550-0](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Special\:BookSources/978-0-231-17550-0). Beginning with Louis Massignon in 1919, it is true that Westerners played a leading role in labeling Islamic modernists as Salafis, even though the term was a misnomer. At the time, European and American scholars felt the need for a useful conceptual box to place Muslim figures such as Jamal al-Din al-Afghani, Muhammad Abduh, and their epigones, all of whom seemed inclined toward a scripturalist understanding of Islam but proved open to rationalism and Western modernity. They chose to adopt salafiyya—a technical term of theology, which they mistook for a reformist slogan and wrongly associated with all kinds of modernist Muslim intellectuals.

[^cite_note-275]: &#x20;Lauziere, Henri (2016). *The Making of Salafism: Islamic Reform in the Twentieth Century*. New York, Chichester, West Sussex: Columbia University Press. p. 237. [ISBN](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ISBN_\(identifier\)) [978-0-231-17550-0](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Special\:BookSources/978-0-231-17550-0). Prior to the fall of the Ottoman Empire, leading reformers who happened to be Salafi in creed were surprisingly open-minded: although they adhered to neo-Hanbali theology. However, the aftermath of the First World War and the expansion of European colonialism paved the way for a series of shifts in thought and attitude. The experiences of Rida offer many examples... he turned against the Shi'is who dared, with reason, to express doubts about the Saudi-Wahhabi project... . Shi'is were not the only victims: Rida and his associates showed their readiness to turn against fellow Salafis who questioned some of the Wahhabis' religious interpretations.

[^cite_note-276]: &#x20;G. Rabil, Robert (2014). *Salafism in Lebanon: From Apoliticism to Transnational Jihadism*. Washington DC, US: Georgetown University Press. pp. 32–33. [ISBN](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ISBN_\(identifier\)) [978-1-62616-116-0](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Special\:BookSources/978-1-62616-116-0). Western colonialists established in these countries political orders... that, even though not professing enmity to Islam and its institutions, left no role for Islam in society. This caused a crisis among Muslim reformists, who felt betrayed not only by the West but also by those nationalists, many of whom were brought to power by the West... Nothing reflects this crisis more than the ideological transformation of Rashid Rida (1865–1935)... He also revived the works of Ibn Taymiyah by publishing his writings and promoting his ideas. Subsequently, taking note of the cataclysmic events brought about by Western policies in the Muslim world and shocked by the abolition of the caliphate, he transformed into a Muslim intellectual mostly concerned about protecting Muslim culture, identity, and politics from Western influence. He supported a theory that essentially emphasized the necessity of an Islamic state in which the scholars of Islam would have a leading role... Rida was a forerunner of Islamist thought. He apparently intended to provide a theoretical platform for a modern Islamic state. His ideas were later incorporated into the works of Islamic scholars. Significantly, his ideas influenced none other than Hassan al-Bannah, founder of the Muslim Brotherhood in Egypt... The Muslim Brethren have taken up Rida's Islamic fundamentalism, a right-wing radical movement founded in 1928,..

[^cite_note-365]: &#x20;Foody, Kathleen (September 2015). Jain, Andrea R. (ed.). ["Interiorizing Islam: Religious Experience and State Oversight in the Islamic Republic of Iran"](https://doi.org/10.1093%2Fjaarel%2Flfv029). *[Journal of the American Academy of Religion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Journal_of_the_American_Academy_of_Religion)*. **83** (3). Oxford: [Oxford University Press](48518.md) on behalf of the [American Academy of Religion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_Academy_of_Religion): 599–623. [doi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Doi_\(identifier\)):[10.1093/jaarel/lfv029](https://doi.org/10.1093%2Fjaarel%2Flfv029). [eISSN](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/EISSN_\(identifier\)) [1477-4585](https://search.worldcat.org/issn/1477-4585). [ISSN](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ISSN_\(identifier\)) [0002-7189](https://search.worldcat.org/issn/0002-7189). [JSTOR](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/JSTOR_\(identifier\)) [24488178](https://www.jstor.org/stable/24488178). [LCCN](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/LCCN_\(identifier\)) [sc76000837](https://lccn.loc.gov/sc76000837). [OCLC](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/OCLC_\(identifier\)) [1479270](https://search.worldcat.org/oclc/1479270). For Shiʿi Muslims, Muhammad not only designated Ali as his friend, but appointed him as his successor—as the "lord" or "master" of the new Muslim community. Ali and his descendants would become known as the Imams, divinely guided leaders of the Shiʿi communities, sinless, and granted special insight into the Qurʾanic text. The theology of the Imams that developed over the next several centuries made little distinction between the authority of the Imams to politically lead the Muslim community and their spiritual prowess; quite to the contrary, their right to political leadership was grounded in their special spiritual insight. While in theory, the only just ruler of the Muslim community was the Imam, the Imams were politically marginal after the first generation. In practice, Shiʿi Muslims negotiated varied approaches to both interpretative authority over Islamic texts and governance of the community, both during the lifetimes of the Imams themselves and even more so following the disappearance of the twelfth and final Imam in the ninth century.
